<span class="atom-split" data-atom="the_two_gentlemen_of_verona" data-title="THE TWO GENTLEMEN OF VERONA" data-chapter="" data-kind="intro"></span>


THE TWO GENTLEMEN OF [[Verona|VERONA]]
THE accomplished elegance of the lyrical verse in The Two Gentlemen of [[Verona]], as [[Well|well]] as the skilful, theatrically effective prose of Lance’s monologues, demonstrates that Shakespeare had already developed his writing skills when he composed this play. Nevertheless—and although the earliest mention of it is by Francis Meres in 1598—it may be his first work for the stage; for its dramatic structure is comparatively unambitious, and while some of its scenes are expertly constructed, those involving more than, at the most, four characters betray an uncertainty of technique suggestive of inexperience. It was first printed in the 1623 Folio.
The [[Friendship|friendship]] of the ‘two gentlemen’—[[Valentine]] and [[Proteus]]—is strained when both fall in [[Love|love]] with [[Silvia]]. [[Proteus]] has followed [[Valentine]] from [[Verona]] to Milan, leaving behind his beloved [[Julia]], who in turn follows him, disguised as a boy. At the climax of the action [[Valentine]] displays the depth of his [[Friendship|friendship]] by offering [[Silvia]] to [[Proteus]]. The conflicting claims of [[Love|love]] and [[Friendship|friendship]] illustrated in this plot had been treated in a considerable [[Body|body]] of English literature written by the [[Time|time]] Shakespeare wrote his play in, or shortly before, 1590. John Lyly’s didactic fiction Euphues (1578) was an immensely popular example; and Lyly’s earliest plays, such as Campaspe (1584) and Endimion (1588), influenced Shakespeare’s style as [[Well|well]] as his subject matter. Shakespeare was writing in a fashionable mode, but his story of [[Proteus]] and [[Julia]] is specifically (though perhaps indirectly) indebted to a prose fiction, [[Diana]], written in Spanish by the Portuguese Jorge de Montemayor and first published in 1559. Many other influences on the young dramatist may be discerned: his idealized portrayal of [[Silvia]] and her relationship with [[Valentine]] derives from the medieval tradition of courtly [[Love|love]]; [[Arthur]] Brooke’s long poem The Tragical [[history|History]] of Romeus and [[Juliet]] (1562) provided some details of the plot; and the comic commentary on the romantic action supplied by the page-boy [[Speed]] and the more rustic clown Lance has dramatic antecedents in English plays such as Lyly’s early comedies.
Though the play was presumably acted in Shakespeare’s [[Time|time]], its first recorded performance is in 1762, in a rewritten version at Drury Lane. Later performances have been sparse, and the play has succeeded best when subjected to adaptation, increasing its musical content, adjusting the emphasis of the last scene so as to reduce the shock of [[Valentine]]’s donation of [[Silvia]] to [[Proteus]], and updating the setting. It can be seen as a dramatic laboratory in which Shakespeare first experimented with conventions of romantic [[Comedy|comedy]] which he would later treat with a more subtle complexity, but it has its own charm. If the whole is not greater than the parts, some of the parts—such as Lance’s brilliant monologues, and the delightful scene (4.2) in which [[Proteus]] serenades his new [[Love|love]] with ‘Who is [[Silvia]]?’ while his disguised old [[Love|love]], [[Julia]], looks wistfully on—are wholly successful. And Lance’s dog, Crab, has the most scene-stealing non-speaking role in the canon: this is an experiment that Shakespeare did not repeat.
THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY
[[Duke of Milan|DUKE of Milan]]
[[Silvia|SILVIA]], his daughter
[[Proteus|PROTEUS]], a gentleman of [[Verona]]
LANCE, his clownish servant
[[Valentine|VALENTINE]], a gentleman of [[Verona]]
[[Speed|SPEED]], his clownish servant
[[Thurio|THURIO]], a foolish rival to [[Valentine]]
[[Antonio|ANTONIO]], father of [[Proteus]]
PANTHINO, his servant
[[Julia|JULIA]], beloved of [[Proteus]]
LUCETTA, her waiting-woman
HOST, where [[Julia]] lodges

EGLAMOUR, agent for [[Silvia]] in her escape

OUTLAWS

Servants, musicians

The Two Gentlemen of [[Verona]]

### Atto 1, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Valentine]] and [[Proteus]] |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Cease to persuade, my loving [[Proteus]].<br>[[Home]]-keeping youth have ever homely wits.<br>Were’t not affection [[Chains\|chains]] thy tender days<br>To the sweet glances of thy honoured [[Love\|love]],<br>I rather would entreat thy company<br>To see the wonders of the world abroad<br>Than, living dully sluggardized at [[Home\|home]],<br>Wear out thy youth with shapeless idleness.<br>But since thou lov’st, [[Love\|love]] still, and thrive therein—<br>Even as I would, when I to [[Love\|love]] begin. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Wilt thou be gone? Sweet [[Valentine]], adieu.<br>Think on thy [[Proteus]] when thou haply seest<br>Some rare noteworthy object in thy travel.<br>Wish me partaker in thy happiness<br>When thou dost meet good hap; and in thy danger—<br>If ever danger do environ thee—<br>Commend thy grievance to my holy prayers;<br>For I will be thy beadsman, [[Valentine]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | And on a [[Love\|love]]-book pray for my [[success]]? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Upon some book I [[Love\|love]] I’ll pray for thee. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | That’s on some shallow story of deep [[Love\|love]]—<br>How young Leander crossed the Hellespont. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | That’s a deep story of a deeper [[Love\|love]],<br>For he was more than over-shoes in [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | ‘Tis true, for you are over-boots in [[Love\|love]],<br>And yet you never swam the Hellespont. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Over the boots? Nay, give me not the boots. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | No, I will not; for it boots thee not. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | What? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | To be in [[Love\|love]], where scorn is bought with groans,<br>Coy looks with heart-sore sighs, one fading moment’s<br>mirth<br>With twenty watchful, weary, tedious nights.<br>If haply won, perhaps a hapless gain;<br>If lost, why then a grievous labour won;<br>However, but a folly bought with wit,<br>Or else a wit by folly vanquished. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | So by your circumstance you call me [[Fool\|fool]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | So by your circumstance I [[Fear\|fear]] you’ll prove. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | ‘Tis [[Love\|love]] you cavil at. I am not [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | [[Love]] is your master, for he masters you,<br>And he that is so yoked by a [[Fool\|fool]]<br>Methinks should not be chronicled for wise. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Yet writers say ‘As in the sweetest bud<br>The eating canker dwells, so doting [[Love\|love]]<br>Inhabits in the finest wits of all.’ |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | And writers say ‘As the most forward bud<br>Is eaten by the canker ere it blow,<br>Even so by [[Love\|love]] the young and tender wit<br>Is turned to folly, blasting in the bud,<br>Losing his verdure even in the prime,<br>And all the fair effects of future hopes.’<br>But wherefore waste I [[Time\|time]] to counsel thee<br>That art a votary to fond [[Desire\|desire]]?<br>Once more adieu. My father at the [[Road\|road]]<br>Expects my coming, there to see me shipped. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | And thither will I bring thee, [[Valentine]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Sweet [[Proteus]], no. Now let us take our leave.<br>To Milan let me hear from thee by letters<br>Of thy [[success]] in [[Love\|love]], and what news else<br>Betideth here in absence of thy friend;<br>And I likewise will visit thee with mine. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | All happiness bechance to thee in Milan. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | As much to you at [[Home\|home]]; and so farewell. Exit |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | He after [[Honour\|honour]] hunts, I after [[Love\|love]].<br>He [[Leaves\|leaves]] his friends to dignify them more,<br>I leave myself, my friends, and all, for [[Love\|love]].<br>Thou, [[Julia]], thou hast metamorphosed me,<br>Made me neglect my studies, lose my [[Time\|time]],<br>War with good counsel, set the world at naught;<br>Made wit with musing weak, heart sick with thought. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Speed]] |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Sir [[Proteus]], save you. Saw you my master? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But now he parted hence to embark for Milan. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Twenty to one, then, he is shipped already,<br>And I have played the sheep in losing him. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Indeed, a sheep doth very often stray,<br>An if the shepherd be a while away. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | You conclude that my master is a shepherd, then,<br>and I a sheep? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I do. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why then, my horns are his horns, whether I wake or [[Sleep\|sleep]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | A silly answer, and fitting [[Well\|well]] a sheep. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | This proves me still a sheep. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | True, and thy master a shepherd. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Nay, that I can deny by a circumstance. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | It shall go hard but I’ll prove it by another. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | The shepherd seeks the sheep, and not the sheep the shepherd. But I seek my master, and my master seeks not me. Therefore I am no sheep. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | The sheep for fodder follow the shepherd, the shepherd for food follows not the sheep. Thou for wages followest thy master, thy master for wages follows not thee. Therefore thou art a sheep. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Such another proof will make me cry ‘baa’. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But dost thou hear: gav’st thou my letter to [[Julia]]? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Ay, sir. I, a lost mutton, gave your letter to her, a laced mutton, and she, a laced mutton, gave me, a lost mutton, nothing for my labour. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Here’s too small a pasture for such store of muttons. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | If the ground be overcharged, you were best stick her. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Nay, in that you are astray. ‘Twere best pound you. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Nay sir, less than a pound shall serve me for carrying your letter. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | You mistake. I mean the pound, a pinfold. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | From a pound to a pin? Fold it over and over ‘Tis threefold too little for carrying a letter to your [[Lover\|lover]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But what said she? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (nods, then says) Ay. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Nod-ay? Why, that’s ‘noddy’. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | You mistook, sir. I say she did nod, and you ask me if she did nod, and I say ‘Ay’. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | And that set together is ‘noddy’. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Now you have taken the pains to set it together, take it for your pains. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | No, no. You shall have it for bearing the letter. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | [[Well]], I perceive I must be fain to bear with you. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Why, sir, how do you bear with me? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Marry, sir, the letter very orderly, having nothing but the word ‘noddy’ for my pains. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Beshrew me but you have a quick wit. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | And yet it cannot overtake your slow purse. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Come, come, open the matter in brief. What said she? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Open your purse, that the money and the matter may be both at once delivered. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | (giving money) [[Well]], sir, here is for your pains. What said she? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Truly, sir, I think you’ll hardly win her. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Why? Couldst thou perceive so much from her? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Sir, I could perceive nothing at all from her, no, not so much as a ducat for delivering your letter. And being so hard to me, that brought your mind, I [[Fear\|fear]] she’ll prove as hard to you in telling your mind. Give her no token but stones, for she’s as hard as steel. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | What said she? Nothing? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | No, not so much as ‘Take this for thy pains’. To testify your bounty, I thank you, you have testerned me; in requital whereof, henceforth carry your letters yourself. And so, sir, I’ll commend you to my master. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Go, go, be gone, to save your [[Ship\|ship]] from wreck,<br>Which cannot perish having thee aboard,<br>Being destined to a drier [[Death\|death]] on shore.<br>I must go send some better messenger.<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] my [[Julia]] would not deign my lines,<br>Receiving them from such a worthless post. Exit |

### Atto 1, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Julia]] and Lucetta |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | But say, Lucetta, now we are [[Alone\|alone]]—<br>Wouldst thou then counsel me to fall in [[Love\|love]]? |
| LUCETTA | Ay, madam, so you stumble not unheedfully. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Of all the fair resort of gentlemen<br>That every day with parle encounter me,<br>In thy opinion which is worthiest [[Love\|love]]? |
| LUCETTA | Please you repeat their names, I’ll show my mind<br>According to my shallow simple skill. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | What think’st thou of the fair Sir Eglamour? |
| LUCETTA | As of a knight [[Well\|well]] spoken, neat, and fine,<br>But were I you, he never should be mine. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | What think’st thou of the rich Mercatio? |
| LUCETTA | [[Well]] of his wealth, but of himself, so-so. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | What think’st thou of the gentle [[Proteus]]? |
| LUCETTA | Lord, lord, to see what folly reigns in us! |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | How now? What means this passion at his name? |
| LUCETTA | Pardon, dear madam, ‘tis a passing shame<br>That I, unworthy [[Body\|body]] as I am,<br>Should censure thus on lovely gentlemen. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Why not on [[Proteus]], as of all the rest? |
| LUCETTA | Then thus: of many good, I think him best. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Your reason? |
| LUCETTA | I have no other but a woman’s reason:<br>I think him so because I think him so. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And wouldst thou have me cast my [[Love\|love]] on him? |
| LUCETTA | Ay, if you thought your [[Love\|love]] not cast away. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Why, he of all the rest hath never moved me. |
| LUCETTA | Yet he of all the rest I think best loves ye. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | His little speaking shows his [[Love\|love]] but small. |
| LUCETTA | [[Fire]] that’s closest kept burns most of all. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | They do not [[Love\|love]] that do not show their [[Love\|love]]. |
| LUCETTA | O, they [[Love\|love]] least that let men know their [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | I would I knew his mind. |
| LUCETTA | (giving [[Proteus]]’ letter)<br>Peruse this paper, madam. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | ‘To [[Julia]]’—say, from whom? |
| LUCETTA | That the contents will show. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Say, say—who gave it thee? |
| LUCETTA | Sir [[Valentine]]’s page; and sent, I think, from [[Proteus]]. He would have given it you, but I being in the way Did in your name receive it. Pardon the fault, I pray. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Now, by my modesty, a goodly broker.<br>Dare you presume to harbour wanton lines?<br>To whisper, and conspire against my youth?<br>Now trust me, ‘tis an office of great worth,<br>And you an officer fit for the place.<br>There. Take the paper.<br>She gives Lucetta the letter<br>See it be returned,<br>Or else return no more into my sight. |
| LUCETTA | To plead for [[Love\|love]] deserves more fee than hate. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Will ye be gone? |
| LUCETTA | That you may ruminate. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And yet I would I had o‘erlooked the letter.<br>It were a shame to call her back again<br>And pray her to a fault for which I chid her.<br>What [[Fool\|fool]] is she, that knows I am a maid<br>And would not force the letter to my view,<br>Since maids in modesty say ‘No’ to that<br>Which they would have the profferer construe ‘Ay’.<br>Fie, fie, how wayward is this foolish [[Love\|love]]<br>That like a testy babe will scratch the [[Nurse\|nurse]]<br>And presently, all humbled, kiss the rod.<br>How churlishly I chid Lucetta hence<br>When willingly I would have had her here.<br>How angerly I taught my brow to frown<br>When inward joy enforced my heart to smile.<br>My penance is to call Lucetta back<br>And ask remission for my folly past.<br>What ho! Lucetta! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lucetta |
| LUCETTA | What would your ladyship? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Is’t near dinner-[[Time\|time]]? |
| LUCETTA | I would it were,<br>That you might kill your stomach on your meat<br>And not upon your maid. |
| *(didascalia)* | She drops and picks up the letter |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | What is’t that you<br>Took up so gingerly? |
| LUCETTA | Nothing. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Why didst thou stoop then? |
| LUCETTA | To take a paper up that I let fall. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And is that paper nothing? |
| LUCETTA | Nothing concerning me. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Then let it lie for those that it concerns. |
| LUCETTA | Madam, it will not lie where it concerns,<br>Unless it have a false interpreter. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Some [[Love\|love]] of yours hath writ to you in rhyme. |
| LUCETTA | That I might sing it, madam, to a tune,<br>Give me a note. Your ladyship can set. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | As little by such toys as may be possible.<br>Best sing it to the tune of ‘Light o’ [[Love\|love]]’. |
| LUCETTA | It is too heavy for so light a tune. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Heavy? Belike it hath some burden, then? |
| LUCETTA | Ay, and melodious were it, would you sing it. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And why not you? |
| LUCETTA | I cannot reach so high. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Let’s see your [[Song\|song]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | She tries to take the letters |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | How now, minion! |
| LUCETTA | Keep tune there still. So you will sing it out.<br>And yet methinks I do not like this tune. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | You do not? |
| LUCETTA | No, madam, ‘tis too sharp. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | You, minion, are too saucy. |
| LUCETTA | Nay, now you are too flat,<br>And mar the concord with too harsh a descant.<br>There wanteth but a mean to fill your [[Song\|song]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | The mean is drowned with your unruly bass. |
| LUCETTA | Indeed, I bid the base for [[Proteus]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | This bauble shall not henceforth trouble me.<br>Here is a coil with protestation.<br>She [[Tears\|tears]] the letter and drops the pieces<br>Go, get you gone, and let the papers lie.<br>You would be fing’ring them to anger me. |
| LUCETTA | (aside)<br>She makes it strange, but she would be best pleased<br>To be so angered with another letter. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Nay, would I were so angered with the same.<br>O hateful hands, to tear such loving words;<br>Injurious wasps, to feed on such sweet honey<br>And kill the [[Bees\|bees]] that yield it with your stings.<br>I’ll kiss each several paper for amends.<br>She picks up some of the pieces of paper<br>Look, here is writ ‘Kind [[Julia]]’—unkind [[Julia]],<br>As in [[Revenge\|revenge]] of thy ingratitude<br>I throw thy name against the bruising stones,<br>Trampling contemptuously on thy disdain.<br>And here is writ ‘[[Love]]-wounded [[Proteus]]’.<br>Poor wounded name, my bosom as a bed<br>Shall lodge thee till thy wound be throughly healed;<br>And thus I search it with a sovereign kiss.<br>But twice or thrice was ‘[[Proteus]]’ written down.<br>Be calm, good [[Wind\|wind]], blow not a word away<br>Till I have found each letter in the letter<br>Except mine own name. That, some whirlwind bear<br>Unto a ragged, fearful, hanging rock<br>And throw it thence into the raging sea.<br>Lo, here in one line is his name twice writ:<br>‘Poor forlorn [[Proteus]]’, ‘passionate [[Proteus]]’,<br>‘To the sweet [[Julia]]’—that I’ll tear away.<br>And yet I will not, sith so prettily<br>He couples it to his complaining names.<br>Thus will I fold them, one upon another.<br>Now kiss, embrace, contend, do what you will. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lucetta |
| LUCETTA | Madam, dinner is ready, and your father stays. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | [[Well]], let us go. |
| LUCETTA | What, shall these papers lie like telltales here? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | If you respect them, best to take them up. |
| LUCETTA | Nay, I was taken up for laying them down.<br>Yet here they shall not lie, for catching cold. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | I see you have a month’s mind to them. |
| LUCETTA | Ay, madam, you may say what sights you see.<br>I see things too, although you judge I wink. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Come, come, will’t please you go? |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Antonio]] and Panthino |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | Tell me, Panthino, what sad talk was that<br>Wherewith my brother held you in the cloister? |
| PANTHINO | ‘Twas of his nephew [[Proteus]], your son. |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | Why, what of him? |
| PANTHINO | He wondered that your lordship<br>Would suffer him to spend his youth at [[Home\|home]]<br>While other men, of slender reputation,<br>Put forth their sons to seek preferment out—<br>Some to the wars, to try their fortune there,<br>Some to discover islands far away,<br>Some to the studious universities.<br>For any or for all these exercises<br>He said that [[Proteus]] your son was meet,<br>And did request me to importune you<br>To let him spend his [[Time\|time]] no more at [[Home\|home]],<br>Which would be great impeachment to his age<br>In having known no travel in his youth. |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | Nor need’st thou much importune me to that<br>Whereon this month I have been hammering.<br>I have considered [[Well\|well]] his loss of [[Time\|time]],<br>And how he cannot be a perfect man,<br>Not being tried and tutored in the world.<br>Experience is by industry achieved,<br>And perfected by the swift course of [[Time\|time]].<br>Then tell me, whither were I best to send him? |
| PANTHINO | I think your lordship is not ignorant<br>How his companion, youthful [[Valentine]],<br>Attends the Emperor in his royal [[Court\|court]]. |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | I know it [[Well\|well]]. |
| PANTHINO | ‘Twere good, I think, your lordship sent him thither.<br>There shall he practise tilts and tournaments,<br>Hear sweet discourse, converse with noblemen,<br>And be in eye of every exercise<br>Worthy his youth and nobleness of birth. |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | I like thy counsel. [[Well]] hast thou advised,<br>And that thou mayst perceive how [[Well\|well]] I like it,<br>The execution of it shall make known.<br>Even with the speediest expedition<br>I will dispatch him to the Emperor’s [[Court\|court]]. |
| PANTHINO | Tomorrow, may it please you, Don Alfonso,<br>With other gentlemen of good esteem,<br>Are journeying to salute the Emperor<br>And to commend their service to his will. |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | Good company. With them shall [[Proteus]] go. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Proteus]] with a letter. He does not see [[Antonio]] and Panthino |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | And in good [[Time\|time]]. Now will we break with him. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Sweet [[Love\|love]], sweet lines, sweet life!<br>Here is her hand, the agent of her heart.<br>Here is her oath for [[Love\|love]], her [[Honour\|honour]]’s pawn.<br>O that our fathers would applaud our loves<br>To seal our happiness with their consents.<br>O heavenly [[Julia]]! |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | How now, what letter are you reading there? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | May’t please your lordship, ‘tis a word or two<br>Of commendations sent from [[Valentine]],<br>Delivered by a friend that came from him. |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | Lend me the letter. Let me see what news. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | There is no news, my lord, but that he writes<br>How happily he lives, how [[Well\|well]] beloved<br>And daily gracèd by the Emperor,<br>Wishing me with him, partner of his fortune. |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | And how stand you affected to his wish? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | As one relying on your lordship’s will,<br>And not depending on his friendly wish. |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | My will is something sorted with his wish.<br>Muse not that I thus suddenly proceed,<br>For what I will, I will, and there an end.<br>I am resolved that thou shalt spend some [[Time\|time]]<br>With Valentinus in the Emperor’s [[Court\|court]].<br>What maintenance he from his friends receives,<br>Like exhibition thou shalt have from me.<br>Tomorrow be in readiness to go.<br>Excuse it not, for I am peremptory. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | My lord, I cannot be so soon provided.<br>Please you deliberate a day or two. |
| [[Antonio\|ANTONIO]] | Look what thou want’st shall be sent after thee.<br>No more of stay. Tomorrow thou must go.<br>Come on, Panthino. You shall be employed<br>To hasten on his expedition. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Antonio]] and Panthino |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Thus have I shunned the [[Fire\|fire]] for [[Fear\|fear]] of burning<br>And drenched me in the sea where I am drowned.<br>I feared to show my father [[Julia]]’s letter<br>Lest he should take exceptions to my [[Love\|love]],<br>And with the vantage of mine own excuse<br>Hath he excepted most against my [[Love\|love]].<br>O, how this [[spring]] of [[Love\|love]] resembleth<br>The uncertain glory of an April day,<br>Which now shows all the [[Beauty\|beauty]] of the sun,<br>And by and by a cloud takes all away. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Panthino |
| PANTHINO | Sir [[Proteus]], your father calls for you.<br>He is in haste, therefore I pray you go. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Why, this it is. My heart accords thereto,<br>And yet a thousand times it answers ‘No’. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Valentine]] and [[Speed]] |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (offering [[Valentine]] a glove)<br>Sir, your glove. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Not mine. My gloves are on. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why then, this may be yours, for this is but one. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Ha, let me see. Ay, give it me, it’s mine—<br>Sweet ornament, that decks a thing divine.<br>Ah, [[Silvia]], [[Silvia]]! |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Madam [[Silvia]], Madam [[Silvia]]! |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | How now, sirrah? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | She is not within hearing, sir. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Why, sir, who bade you call her? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Your worship, sir, or else I mistook. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | [[Well]], you’ll still be too forward. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | And yet I was last chidden for being too slow. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Go to, sir. Tell me, do you know Madam [[Silvia]]? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | She that your worship loves? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Why, how know you that I am in [[Love\|love]]? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Marry, by these special marks: first, you have learned, like Sir [[Proteus]], to wreath your arms, like a malcontent; to relish a [[Love\|love]]-[[Song\|song]], like a robin redbreast; to walk [[Alone\|alone]], like one that had the pestilence; to sigh, like a schoolboy that had lost his ABC; to weep, like a young wench that had buried her grandam; to fast, like one that takes diet; to watch, like one that fears robbing; to speak puling, like a beggar at Hallowmas. You were wont, when you laughed, to crow like a cock; when you walked, to walk like one of the lions. When you fasted, it was presently after dinner; when you looked sadly, it was for want of money. And now you are metamorphosed with a mistress, that when I look on you I can hardly think you my master. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Are all these things perceived in me? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | They are all perceived without ye. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Without me? They cannot. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Without you? Nay, that’s certain, for without you were so simple, none else would. But you are so without these follies that these follies are within you, and shine through you like the [[Water\|water]] in an urinal, that not an eye that sees you but is a physician to comment on your malady. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | But tell me, dost thou know my lady [[Silvia]]? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | She that you gaze on so as she sits at supper? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Hast thou observed that? Even she I mean. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why sir, I know her not. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Dost thou know her by my gazing on her, and yet know’st her not? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Is she not hard-favoured, sir? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Not so fair, boy, as [[Well\|well]] favoured. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Sir, I know that [[Well\|well]] enough. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | What dost thou know? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | That she is not so fair as of you [[Well\|well]] favoured. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I mean that her [[Beauty\|beauty]] is exquisite but her favour infinite. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | That’s because the one is painted and the other out of all count. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | How painted? And how out of count? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Marry, sir, so painted to make her fair that no man counts of her [[Beauty\|beauty]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | How esteem’st thou me? I account of her [[Beauty\|beauty]]. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | You never saw her since she was deformed. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | How long hath she been deformed? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Ever since you loved her. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I have loved her ever since I saw her, and still I see her beautiful. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | If you [[Love\|love]] her you cannot see her. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Why? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Because [[Love\|love]] is blind. O that you had mine eyes, or your own eyes had the lights they were wont to have when you chid at Sir [[Proteus]] for going ungartered. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | What should I see then? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Your own present folly and her passing deformity; for he being in [[Love\|love]] could not see to garter his hose, and you being in [[Love\|love]] cannot see to put on your hose. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Belike, boy, then you are in [[Love\|love]], for last morning you could not see to wipe my shoes. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | True, sir. I was in [[Love\|love]] with my bed. I thank you, you swinged me for my [[Love\|love]], which makes me the bolder to chide you for yours. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | In conclusion, I stand affected to her. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | I would you were set. So your affection would cease. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Last [[Night\|night]] she enjoined me to write some lines to one she loves. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | And have you? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I have. 85 |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Are they not lamely writ? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | No, boy, but as [[Well\|well]] as I can do them. Peace, here she comes. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Silvia]] |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (aside) O excellent motion! O exceeding puppet!<br>Now will he interpret to her. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Madam and mistress, a thousand good-morrows. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (aside)<br>O, give ye good e’en! Here’s a million of manners. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Sir [[Valentine]] and servant, to you two thousand. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (aside) He should give her interest, and she gives it him. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | As you enjoined me, I have writ your letter<br>Unto the secret, nameless friend of yours;<br>Which I was much unwilling to proceed in<br>But for my duty to your ladyship.<br>He gives her a letter |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | I thank you, gentle servant. ‘Tis very clerkly done. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Now trust me, madam, it came hardly off;<br>For being ignorant to whom it goes<br>I writ at random, very doubtfully. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Perchance you think too much of so much pains? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | No, madam. So it stead you I will write—<br>Please you command—a thousand times as much.<br>And yet . . . |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | A pretty period. [[Well]], I guess the sequel.<br>And yet I will not name it. And yet I care not.<br>And yet, take this again.<br>She offers him the letter<br>And yet I thank you,<br>Meaning henceforth to trouble you no more. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (aside)<br>And yet you will, and yet another yet. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | What means your ladyship? Do you not like it? |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Yes, yes. The lines are very quaintly writ,<br>But since unwillingly, take them again.<br>She presses the letter upon him<br>Nay, take them. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Madam, they are for you. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Ay, ay. You writ them, sir, at my request,<br>But I will none of them. They are for you.<br>I would have had them writ more movingly. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Please you, I’ll write your ladyship another. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | And when it’s writ, for my sake read it over,<br>And if it please you, so. If not, why, so. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | If it please me, madam? What then? |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Why, if it please you, take it for your labour.<br>And so good morrow, servant. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (aside)<br>O jest unseen, inscrutable, invisible<br>As a nose on a man’s face or a weathercock on a<br>steeple.<br>My master sues to her, and she hath taught her suitor,<br>He being her pupil, to become her tutor.<br>O excellent device! Was there ever heard a better?—<br>That my master, being scribe, to himself should write<br>the letter. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | How now, sir—what, are you reasoning with yourself? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Nay, I was rhyming. ‘Tis you that have the reason. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | To do what? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | To be a spokesman from Madam [[Silvia]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | To whom? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | To yourself. Why, she woos you by a figure. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | What figure? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | By a letter, I should say. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Why, she hath not writ to me. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | What need she, when she hath made you write to yourself? Why, do you not perceive the jest? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | No, believe me. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | No believing you indeed, sir. But did you perceive her earnest? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | She gave me none, except an angry word. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why, she hath given you a letter. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | That’s the letter I writ to her friend. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | And that letter hath she delivered, and there an end. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I would it were no worse. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | I’ll warrant you, ‘tis as [[Well\|well]].<br>For often have you writ to her, and she in modesty<br>Or else for want of idle [[Time\|time]] could not again reply,<br>Or fearing else some messenger that might her mind<br>discover,<br>Herself hath taught her [[Love\|love]] himself to write unto her<br>[[Lover\|lover]].<br>—All this I speak in print, for in print I found it. Why<br>muse you, sir? ‘Tis dinner-[[Time\|time]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I have dined. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Ay, but hearken, sir. Though the chameleon [[Love\|love]] can feed on the air, I am one that am nourished by my victuals, and would fain have meat. O, be not like your mistress—be moved, be moved! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Proteus]] and [[Julia]] |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Have patience, gentle [[Julia]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | I must where is no remedy. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | When possibly I can I will return. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | If you turn not, you will return the sooner.<br>She gives him a ring<br>Keep this remembrance for thy [[Julia]]’s sake. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Why then, we’ll make exchange. Here, take you this.<br>He gives her a ring |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And seal the bargain with a holy kiss. |
| *(didascalia)* | They kiss |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Here is my hand for my true constancy.<br>And when that hour o’erslips me in the day<br>Wherein I sigh not, [[Julia]], for thy sake,<br>The next ensuing hour some foul mischance<br>Torment me for my [[Love\|love]]’s forgetfulness.<br>My father stays my coming. Answer not.<br>The tide is now. ([[Julia]] weeps) Nay, not thy tide of [[Tears\|tears]],<br>That tide will stay me longer than I should.<br>[[Julia]], farewell. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[Julia]] |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | What, gone without a word?<br>Ay, so true [[Love\|love]] should do. It cannot speak,<br>For [[Truth\|truth]] hath better deeds than words to grace it. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Panthino |
| PANTHINO | Sir [[Proteus]], you are stayed for. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Go, I come, I come.—<br>Alas, this parting strikes poor [[lovers]] dumb. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lance with his dog Crab |
| LANCE | (to the audience) Nay, ‘twill be this hour ere I have done weeping. All the kind of the Lances have this very fault. I have received my proportion, like the prodigious son, and am going with Sir [[Proteus]] to the Imperial’s [[Court\|court]]. I think Crab, my dog, be the sourest-natured dog that lives. My mother weeping, my father wailing, my sister crying, our maid howling, our cat wringing her hands, and all our [[house]] in a great perplexity, yet did not this cruel-hearted cur shed one tear. He is a stone, a very pebble-stone, and has no more pity in him than a dog. A Jew would have wept to have seen our parting. Why, my grandam, having no eyes, look you, wept herself blind at my parting. Nay, I’ll show you the manner of it. This shoe is my father. No, this left shoe is my father. No, no, this left shoe is my mother. Nay, that cannot be so, neither. Yes, it is so, it is so, it hath the worser sole. This shoe with the hole in it is my mother, and this my father. A vengeance on’t, there ‘tis. Now, sir, this staff is my sister, for, look you, she is as white as a lily and as small as a wand. This hat is Nan our maid. I am the dog. No, the dog is himself, and I am the dog. O, the dog is me, and I am myself. Ay, so, so. Now come I to my father. ‘Father, your blessing.’ Now should not the shoe speak a word for weeping. Now should I kiss my father. [[Well]], he weeps on. Now come I to my mother. O that she could speak now, like a moved woman. [[Well]], I kiss her. Why, there ‘tis. Here’s my mother’s breath up and down. Now come I to my sister. Mark the moan she makes.—Now the dog all this while sheds not a tear nor speaks a word. But see how I lay the [[Dust\|dust]] with my [[Tears\|tears]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Panthino |
| PANTHINO | Lance, away, away, aboard. Thy master is shipped, and thou art to post after with oars. What’s the matter? Why weep’st thou, man? Away, ass, you’ll lose the tide if you tarry any longer. |
| LANCE | It is no matter if the tied were lost, for it is the unkindest tied that ever any man tied. |
| PANTHINO | What’s the unkindest tide? |
| LANCE | Why, he that’s tied here, Crab my dog. |
| PANTHINO | Tut, man, I mean thou’lt lose the flood, and in losing the flood, lose thy voyage, and in losing thy voyage, lose thy master, and in losing thy master, lose thy service, and in losing thy service—<br>Lance puts his hand over Panthino’s mouth<br>Why dost thou stop my mouth? |
| LANCE | For [[Fear\|fear]] thou shouldst lose thy tongue. |
| PANTHINO | Where should I lose my tongue? |
| LANCE | In thy tale. |
| PANTHINO | In thy tail! |
| LANCE | Lose the tide, and the voyage, and the master, and the service, and the tied? Why, man, if the [[River\|river]] were dry, I am able to fill it with my [[Tears\|tears]]. If the [[Wind\|wind]] were down, I could drive the boat with my sighs. |
| PANTHINO | Come, come away, man. I was sent to call thee. |
| LANCE | Sir, call me what thou darest. |
| PANTHINO | Wilt thou go? |
| LANCE | [[Well]], I will go. Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Valentine]], [[Silvia]], [[Thurio]], and [[Speed]] |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Servant! |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Mistress? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (to [[Valentine]]) Master, Sir [[Thurio]] frowns on you. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Ay, boy, it’s for [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Not of you. 5 |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Of my mistress, then. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Twere good you knocked him. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | (to [[Valentine]]) Servant, you are sad. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Indeed, madam, I seem so. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Seem you that you are not? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Haply I do. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | So do counterfeits. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | So do you. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | What seem I that I am not? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Wise. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | What instance of the contrary? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Your folly. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | And how quote you my folly? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I quote it in your jerkin. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | My ‘jerkin’ is a doublet. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | [[Well]] then, I’ll [[Double\|double]] your folly. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | How! |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | What, angry, Sir [[Thurio]]? Do you change colour? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Give him leave, madam, he is a kind of chameleon. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | That hath more mind to feed on your [[Blood\|blood]] than live in your air. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | You have said, sir. |
| THORIO | Ay, sir, and done too, for this [[Time\|time]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I know it [[Well\|well]], sir, you always end ere you begin. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | A fine volley of words, gentlemen, and quickly shot off. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | ‘Tis indeed, madam, we thank the giver. [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] Who is that, servant? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Yourself, sweet lady, for you gave the [[Fire\|fire]]. Sir [[Thurio]] borrows his wit from your ladyship’s looks, and spends what he borrows kindly in your company. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Sir, if you spend word for word with me, I shall make your wit bankrupt. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I know it [[Well\|well]], sir. You have an exchequer of words, and, I think, no other treasure to give your followers. For it appears by their bare liveries that they live by your bare words. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | No more, gentlemen, no more. Here comes my father. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Duke |
| DUKE | Now, daughter [[Silvia]], you are hard beset.<br>Sir [[Valentine]], your father is in good health,<br>What say you to a letter from your friends<br>Of much good news? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | My lord, I will be thankful<br>To any happy messenger from thence. |
| DUKE | Know ye Don [[Antonio]], your countryman? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Ay, my good lord, I know the gentleman<br>To be of worth, and worthy estimation,<br>And not without [[Desert\|desert]] so [[Well\|well]] reputed. |
| DUKE | Hath he not a son? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Ay, my good lord, a son that [[Well\|well]] deserves<br>The [[Honour\|honour]] and regard of such a father. |
| DUKE | You know him [[Well\|well]]? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I knew him as myself, for from our infancy<br>We have conversed, and spent our hours together.<br>And though myself have been an idle truant,<br>Omitting the sweet benefit of [[Time\|time]]<br>To clothe mine age with angel-like perfection,<br>Yet hath Sir [[Proteus]]—for that’s his name—<br>Made use and fair advantage of his days:<br>His years but young, but his experience old;<br>His head unmellowed, but his judgement ripe.<br>And in a word—for far behind his worth<br>Comes all the praises that I now bestow—<br>He is complete, in feature and in mind,<br>With all good grace to grace a gentleman. |
| DUKE | Beshrew me, sir, but if he make this good<br>He is as worthy for an empress’ [[Love\|love]]<br>As meet to be an emperor’s counsellor.<br>[[Well]], sir, this gentleman is come to me<br>With commendation from great potentates,<br>And here he means to spend his [[Time\|time]] awhile.<br>I think ’tis no unwelcome news to you. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Should I have wished a thing it had been he. |
| DUKE | Welcome him then according to his worth.<br>[[Silvia]], I speak to you, and you, Sir [[Thurio]];<br>For [[Valentine]], I need not cite him to it.<br>I will send him hither to you presently. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | This is the gentleman I told your ladyship<br>Had come along with me, but that his mistress<br>Did hold his eyes locked in her crystal looks. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Belike that now she hath enfranchised them<br>Upon some other pawn for fealty. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Nay, sure, I think she holds them prisoners still. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Nay, then he should be blind, and being blind<br>How could he see his way to seek out you? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Why, lady, [[Love\|love]] hath twenty pair of eyes. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | They say that [[Love\|love]] hath not an eye at all. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | To see such [[lovers]], [[Thurio]], as yourself.<br>Upon a homely object [[Love\|love]] can wink. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Have done, have done. Here comes the gentleman. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Proteus]] |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Welcome, dear [[Proteus]]. Mistress, I beseech you<br>Confirm his welcome with some special favour. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | His worth is warrant for his welcome hither,<br>If this be he you oft have wished to hear from. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Mistress, it is. Sweet lady, entertain him<br>To be my fellow-servant to your ladyship. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Too low a mistress for so high a servant. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Not so, sweet lady, but too mean a servant<br>To have a look of such a worthy mistress. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Leave off discourse of disability.<br>Sweet lady, entertain him for your servant. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | My duty will I boast of, nothing else. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | And duty never yet did want his meed.<br>Servant, you are welcome to a worthless mistress. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I’ll die on him that says so but yourself. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | That you are welcome? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | That you are worthless. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Servant |
| *(didascalia)* | SERVANT |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Madam, my lord your father would speak with you. |
| *(didascalia)* | I wait upon his pleasure. Exit the Servant |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Come, Sir [[Thurio]],<br>Go with me. Once more, new servant, welcome.<br>I’ll leave you to confer of [[Home\|home]] affairs.<br>When you have done, we look to hear from you. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | We’ll both attend upon your ladyship. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Silvia]] and [[Thurio]] |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Now tell me, how do all from whence you came? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Your friends are [[Well\|well]], and have them much commended. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | And how do yours? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I left them all in health. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | How does your lady, and how thrives your [[Love\|love]]? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | My tales of [[Love\|love]] were wont to weary you.<br>I know you joy not in a [[Love\|love]]-discourse. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Ay, [[Proteus]], but that life is altered now.<br>I have done penance for contemning [[Love\|love]],<br>Whose high imperious thoughts have punished me<br>With bitter fasts, with penitential groans,<br>With nightly [[Tears\|tears]] and daily heart-sore sighs.<br>For in [[Revenge\|revenge]] of my contempt of [[Love\|love]]<br>[[Love]] hath chased [[Sleep\|sleep]] from my enthralled eyes,<br>And made them watchers of mine own heart’s sorrow.<br>O gentle [[Proteus]], [[Love\|love]]’s a mighty lord,<br>And hath so humbled me as I confess<br>There is no woe to his correction,<br>Nor to his service no such joy on earth.<br>Now, no discourse except it be of [[Love\|love]].<br>Now can I break my fast, dine, sup, and [[Sleep\|sleep]]<br>Upon the very naked name of [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Enough. I read your fortune in your eye.<br>Was this the idol that you worship so? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Even she; and is she not a heavenly saint? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | No, but she is an earthly paragon. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Call her divine. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I will not flatter her. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | O flatter me; for [[Love\|love]] delights in praises. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | When I was sick you gave me bitter pills,<br>And I must minister the like to you. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Then speak the [[Truth\|truth]] by her; if not divine,<br>Yet let her be a principality,<br>Sovereign to all the creatures on the earth. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Except my mistress. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Sweet, except not any,<br>Except thou wilt except against my [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Have I not reason to prefer mine own? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | And I will help thee to prefer her, too.<br>She shall be dignified with this high [[Honour\|honour]],<br>To bear my lady’s train, lest the base earth<br>Should from her vesture chance to steal a kiss<br>And, of so great a favour growing proud,<br>Disdain to root the [[Summer\|summer]]-swelling flower,<br>And make rough [[Winter\|winter]] everlastingly. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Why, [[Valentine]], what braggartism is this? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Pardon me, [[Proteus]], all I can is nothing<br>To her whose worth makes other worthies nothing.<br>She is [[Alone\|alone]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Then let her [[Alone\|alone]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Not for the world. Why man, she is mine own,<br>And I as rich in having such a [[Jewel\|jewel]]<br>As twenty seas, if all their sand were pearl,<br>The [[Water\|water]] nectar, and the rocks pure gold.<br>Forgive me that I do not dream on thee<br>Because thou seest me dote upon my [[Love\|love]].<br>My foolish rival, that her father likes<br>Only for his possessions are so huge,<br>Is gone with her along, and I must after;<br>For [[Love\|love]], thou know’st, is full of [[Jealousy\|jealousy]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But she loves you? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Ay, and we are betrothed. Nay more, our [[Marriage\|marriage]]<br>hour,<br>With all the cunning manner of our flight,<br>Determined of: how I must climb her [[Window\|window]],<br>The ladder made of cords, and all the means<br>Plotted and ‘[[Greed\|greed]] on for my happiness.<br>Good [[Proteus]], go with me to my chamber<br>In these affairs to aid me with thy counsel. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Go on before. I shall enquire you forth.<br>I must unto the [[Road\|road]], to disembark<br>Some necessaries that I needs must use,<br>And then I’ll presently attend you. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Will you make haste? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I will. Exit [[Valentine]]<br>Even as one heat another heat expels,<br>Or as one nail by strength drives out another,<br>So the remembrance of my former [[Love\|love]]<br>Is by a newer object quite forgotten.<br>Is it mine eye, or [[Valentine]]’s praise,<br>Her true perfection, or my false transgression<br>That makes me, reasonless, to reason thus?<br>She is fair, and so is [[Julia]] that I [[Love\|love]]—<br>That I did [[Love\|love]], for now my [[Love\|love]] is thawed,<br>Which like a waxen image ‘gainst a [[Fire\|fire]]<br>Bears no impression of the thing it was.<br>Methinks my zeal to [[Valentine]] is cold,<br>And that I [[Love\|love]] him not as I was wont.<br>O, but I [[Love\|love]] his lady too-too much,<br>And that’s the reason I [[Love\|love]] him so little.<br>How shall I dote on her with more advice,<br>That thus without advice begin to [[Love\|love]] her?<br>‘Tis but her picture I have yet beheld,<br>And that hath dazzled my reason’s light.<br>But when I look on her perfections<br>There is no reason but I shall be blind.<br>If I can check my erring [[Love\|love]] I will,<br>If not, to compass her I’ll use my skill. Exit |

### Atto 2, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Speed]], and Lance with his dog Crab |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Lance, by mine honesty, welcome to Milan. |
| LANCE | Forswear not thyself, sweet youth, for I am not welcome. I reckon this always, that a man is never undone till he be hanged, nor never welcome to a place till some certain shot be paid and the hostess say ‘Welcome’. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Come on, you madcap. I’ll to the alehouse with you presently, where, for one shot of five pence, thou shalt have five thousand welcomes. But sirrah, how did thy master part with Madam [[Julia]]? |
| LANCE | Marry, after they closed in earnest they parted very fairly in jest. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | But shall she marry him? |
| LANCE | No. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | How then, shall he marry her? |
| LANCE | No, neither. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | What, are they broken? |
| LANCE | No, they are both as whole as a fish. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why then, how stands the matter with them? |
| LANCE | Marry, thus: when it stands [[Well\|well]] with him it stands [[Well\|well]] with her. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | What an ass art thou! I understand thee not. |
| LANCE | What a block art thou, that thou canst not! My staff understands me. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | What thou sayst? |
| LANCE | Ay, and what I do too. Look thee, I’ll but lean, and my staff under-stands me. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | It stands under thee indeed. |
| LANCE | Why, stand-under and under-stand is all one. [[Speed\|SPEED]] But tell me true, will’t be a match? |
| LANCE | Ask my dog. If he say ‘Ay’, it will. If he say ‘No’, it will. If he shake his tail and say nothing, it will. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | The conclusion is, then, that it will. |
| LANCE | Thou shalt never get such a secret from me but by a [[Parable\|parable]]. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Tis [[Well\|well]] that I get it so. But Lance, how sayst thou that my master is become a notable [[Lover\|lover]]? |
| LANCE | I never knew him otherwise. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Than how? |
| LANCE | A notable lubber, as thou reportest him to be. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why, thou whoreson ass, thou mistak’st me. |
| LANCE | Why, [[Fool\|fool]], I meant not thee, I meant thy master. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | I tell thee my master is become a hot [[Lover\|lover]]. |
| LANCE | Why, I tell thee I care not, though he burn himself in [[Love\|love]]. If thou wilt, go with me to the alehouse. If not, thou art an Hebrew, a Jew, and not worth the name of a Christian. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why? |
| LANCE | Because thou hast not so much [[Charity\|charity]] in thee as to go to the ale with a Christian. Wilt thou go? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | At thy service. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Proteus]] |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | To leave my [[Julia]] shall I be forsworn;<br>To [[Love\|love]] fair [[Silvia]] shall I be forsworn;<br>To wrong my friend I shall be much forsworn.<br>And e‘en that power which gave me first my oath<br>Provokes me to this threefold perjury.<br>[[Love]] bade me swear, and [[Love\|love]] bids me forswear.<br>O sweet-suggesting [[Love\|love]], if thou hast sinned<br>Teach me, thy tempted subject, to excuse it.<br>At first I did adore a twinkling star,<br>But now I worship a celestial sun.<br>Unheedful vows may heedfully be broken,<br>And he wants wit that wants resolved will<br>To learn his wit t’exchange the bad for better.<br>Fie, fie, unreverent tongue, to call her bad<br>Whose sovereignty so oft thou hast preferred<br>With twenty thousand [[Soul\|soul]]-confirming oaths.<br>I cannot leave to [[Love\|love]], and yet I do.<br>But there I leave to [[Love\|love]] where I should [[Love\|love]].<br>[[Julia]] I lose, and [[Valentine]] I lose.<br>If I keep them I needs must lose myself.<br>If I lose them, thus find I by their loss<br>For [[Valentine]], myself, for [[Julia]], [[Silvia]].<br>I to myself am dearer than a friend,<br>For [[Love\|love]] is still most precious in itself,<br>And [[Silvia]]—witness heaven that made her fair—<br>Shows [[Julia]] but a swarthy Ethiope.<br>I will forget that [[Julia]] is alive,<br>Rememb’ring that my [[Love\|love]] to her is dead,<br>And [[Valentine]] I’ll hold an enemy,<br>Aiming at [[Silvia]] as a sweeter friend.<br>I cannot now prove constant to myself<br>Without some treachery used to [[Valentine]].<br>This [[Night\|night]] he meaneth with a corded ladder<br>To climb celestial [[Silvia]]’s chamber-[[Window\|window]],<br>Myself in counsel his competitor.<br>Now presently I’ll give her father notice<br>Of their disguising and pretended flight,<br>Who, all enraged, will banish [[Valentine]];<br>For [[Thurio]] he intends shall wed his daughter.<br>But [[Valentine]] being gone, I’ll quickly [[Cross\|cross]]<br>By some sly trick blunt [[Thurio]]’s dull proceeding.<br>[[Love]], lend me wings to make my purpose swift,<br>As thou hast lent me wit to plot this drift. Exit |

### Atto 2, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Julia]] and Lucetta |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Counsel, Lucetta. Gentle girl, assist me,<br>And e’en in kind [[Love\|love]] I do conjure thee,<br>Who art the table wherein all my thoughts<br>Are visibly charactered and engraved,<br>To lesson me, and tell me some good mean<br>How with my [[Honour\|honour]] I may undertake<br>A [[Journey\|journey]] to my loving [[Proteus]]. |
| LUCETTA | Alas, the way is wearisome and long. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | A true-devoted [[Pilgrim\|pilgrim]] is not weary<br>To measure kingdoms with his feeble steps.<br>Much less shall she that hath [[Love\|love]]’s wings to fly,<br>And when the flight is made to one so dear,<br>Of such divine perfection as Sir [[Proteus]]. |
| LUCETTA | Better forbear till [[Proteus]] make return. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | O, know’st thou not his looks are my [[Soul\|soul]]’s food?<br>Pity the dearth that I have pined in<br>By [[Longing\|longing]] for that food so long a [[Time\|time]].<br>Didst thou but know the inly touch of [[Love\|love]]<br>Thou wouldst as soon go kindle [[Fire\|fire]] with [[Snow\|snow]]<br>As seek to quench the [[Fire\|fire]] of [[Love\|love]] with words. |
| LUCETTA | I do not seek to quench your [[Love\|love]]’s hot [[Fire\|fire]],<br>But qualify the [[Fire\|fire]]’s extreme rage,<br>Lest it should burn above the bounds of reason. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | The more thou damm‘st it up, the more it burns.<br>The current that with gentle murmur glides,<br>Thou know’st, being stopped, impatiently doth rage.<br>But when his fair course is not hindered<br>He makes sweet music with th’enamelled stones,<br>Giving a gentle kiss to every sedge<br>He overtaketh in his pilgrimage.<br>And so by many winding nooks he strays<br>With willing sport to the wild ocean.<br>Then let me go, and hinder not my course.<br>I’ll be as patient as a gentle stream,<br>And make a pastime of each weary step<br>Till the last step have brought me to my [[Love\|love]].<br>And there I’ll rest as after much turmoil<br>A blessed [[Soul\|soul]] doth in Elysium. |
| LUCETTA | But in what habit will you go along? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Not like a woman, for I would prevent<br>The loose encounters of lascivious men.<br>Gentle Lucetta, fit me with such weeds<br>As may beseem some [[Well\|well]]-reputed page. |
| LUCETTA | Why then, your ladyship must cut your hair. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | No, girl, I’ll knit it up in silken strings<br>With twenty odd-conceited true-[[Love\|love]] knots.<br>To be fantastic may become a youth<br>Of greater [[Time\|time]] than I shall show to be. |
| LUCETTA | What fashion, madam, shall I make your breeches? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | That fits as [[Well\|well]] as ‘Tell me, good my lord,<br>What compass will you wear your farthingale?’<br>Why, e’en what fashion thou best likes, Lucetta. |
| LUCETTA | You must needs have them with a codpiece, madam. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Out, out, Lucetta. That will be ill-favoured. |
| LUCETTA | A round hose, madam, now’s not worth a pin<br>Unless you have a codpiece to stick pins on. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Lucetta, as thou lov‘st me let me have<br>What thou think’st meet and is most mannerly.<br>But tell me, wench, how will the world repute me<br>For undertaking so unstaid a [[Journey\|journey]]?<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] me it will make me scandalized. |
| LUCETTA | If you think so, then stay at [[Home\|home]], and go not. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Nay, that I will not. |
| LUCETTA | Then never dream on infamy, but go.<br>If [[Proteus]] like your [[Journey\|journey]] when you come,<br>No matter who’s displeased when you are gone.<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] me he will scarce be pleased withal. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | That is the least, Lucetta, of my [[Fear\|fear]].<br>A thousand oaths, an ocean of his [[Tears\|tears]],<br>And instances of infinite of [[Love\|love]]<br>Warrant me welcome to my [[Proteus]]. |
| LUCETTA | All these are servants to deceitful men. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Base men, that use them to so base effect.<br>But truer [[Stars\|stars]] did govern [[Proteus]]’ birth.<br>His words are bonds, his oaths are oracles,<br>His [[Love\|love]] sincere, his thoughts immaculate,<br>His [[Tears\|tears]] pure messengers sent from his heart,<br>His heart as far from fraud as heaven from earth. |
| LUCETTA | Pray heaven he prove so when you come to him. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Now, as thou lov’st me, do him not that wrong<br>To bear a hard opinion of his [[Truth\|truth]].<br>Only deserve my [[Love\|love]] by loving him,<br>And presently go with me to my chamber<br>To take a note of what I stand in need of<br>To furnish me upon my [[Longing\|longing]] [[Journey\|journey]].<br>All that is mine I leave at thy dispose,<br>My goods, my lands, my reputation;<br>Only in lieu thereof dispatch me hence.<br>Come, answer not, but to it presently.<br>I am impatient of my tarriance. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Duke, [[Thurio]], and [[Proteus]] |
| DUKE | Sir [[Thurio]], give us leave, I pray, awhile.<br>We have some [[Secrets\|secrets]] to confer about. Exit [[Thurio]]<br><br>Now tell me, [[Proteus]], what’s your will with me? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | My gracious lord, that which I would discover<br>The law of [[Friendship\|friendship]] bids me to conceal.<br>But when I call to mind your gracious favours<br>Done to me, undeserving as I am,<br>My duty pricks me on to utter that<br>Which else no worldly good should draw from me.<br>Know, worthy prince, Sir [[Valentine]] my friend<br>This [[Night\|night]] intends to steal away your daughter.<br>Myself am one made privy to the plot.<br>I know you have determined to bestow her<br>On [[Thurio]], whom your gentle daughter hates,<br>And should she thus be stol’n away from you<br>It would be much vexation to your age.<br>Thus, for my duty’s sake, I rather chose<br>To [[Cross\|cross]] my friend in his intended drift<br>Than by concealing it heap on your head<br>A pack of sorrows which would press you down,<br>Being unprevented, to your timeless [[Grave\|grave]]. |
| DUKE | [[Proteus]], I thank thee for thine honest care,<br>Which to requite command me while I live.<br>This [[Love\|love]] of theirs myself have often seen,<br>Haply, when they have judged me fast asleep,<br>And oftentimes have purposed to forbid<br>Sir [[Valentine]] her company and my [[Court\|court]].<br>But fearing lest my jealous aim might err,<br>And so unworthily disgrace the man—<br>A rashness that I ever yet have shunned—<br>I gave him gentle looks, thereby to find<br>That which thyself hast now disclosed to me.<br>And that thou mayst perceive my [[Fear\|fear]] of this,<br>Knowing that tender youth is soon suggested,<br>I nightly lodge her in an upper tower,<br>The key whereof myself have ever kept;<br>And thence she cannot be conveyed away. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Know, noble lord, they have devised a mean<br>How he her chamber-[[Window\|window]] will ascend,<br>And with a corded ladder fetch her down,<br>For which the youthful [[Lover\|lover]] now is gone,<br>And this way comes he with it presently,<br>Where, if it please you, you may intercept him.<br>But, good my lord, do it so cunningly<br>That my discovery be not aimed at;<br>For [[Love\|love]] of you, not hate unto my friend,<br>Hath made me publisher of this pretence. |
| DUKE | Upon mine [[Honour\|honour]], he shall never know<br>That I had any light from thee of this. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Adieu, my lord. Sir [[Valentine]] is coming. Exit Enter [[Valentine]] |
| DUKE | Sir [[Valentine]], whither away so fast? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Please it your grace, there is a messenger<br>That stays to bear my letters to my friends,<br>And I am going to deliver them. |
| DUKE | Be they of much import? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | The tenor of them doth but signify<br>My health and happy being at your [[Court\|court]]. |
| DUKE | Nay then, no matter. Stay with me awhile.<br>I am to break with thee of some affairs<br>That touch me near, wherein thou must be secret.<br>‘Tis not unknown to thee that I have sought<br>To match my friend Sir [[Thurio]] to my daughter. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I know it [[Well\|well]], my lord; and sure the match<br>Were rich and honourable. Besides, the gentleman<br>Is full of virtue, bounty, worth, and qualities<br>Beseeming such a wife as your fair daughter.<br>Cannot your grace win her to fancy him? |
| DUKE | No, trust me. She is peevish, sullen, froward,<br>Proud, disobedient, stubborn, lacking duty,<br>Neither regarding that she is my [[Child\|child]]<br>Nor fearing me as if I were her father.<br>And may I say to thee, this [[Pride\|pride]] of hers<br>Upon advice hath drawn my [[Love\|love]] from her,<br>And where I thought the remnant of mine age<br>Should have been cherished by her [[Child\|child]]-like duty,<br>I now am full resolved to take a wife,<br>And turn her out to who will take her in.<br>Then let her [[Beauty\|beauty]] be her wedding dower,<br>For me and my possessions she esteems not. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | What would your grace have me to do in this? |
| DUKE | There is a lady of [[Verona]] here<br>Whom I affect, but she is nice, and coy,<br>And naught esteems my aged eloquence.<br>Now therefore would I have thee to my tutor—<br>For long agone I have forgot to [[Court\|court]],<br>Besides, the fashion of the [[Time\|time]] is changed—<br>How and which way I may bestow myself<br>To be regarded in her sun-bright eye. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Win her with gifts if she respect not words.<br>Dumb jewels often in their silent kind<br>More than quick words do move a woman’s mind. |
| DUKE | But she did scorn a present that I sent her. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | A woman sometime scorns what best contents her.<br>Send her another. Never give her o’er,<br>For scorn at first makes after-[[Love\|love]] the more.<br>If she do frown, ‘tis not in hate of you,<br>But rather to beget more [[Love\|love]] in you.<br>If she do chide, ‘tis not to have you gone,<br>Forwhy the fools are mad if left [[Alone\|alone]].<br>Take no repulse, whatever she doth say:<br>For ‘Get you gone’ she doth not mean ‘Away’.<br>Flatter and praise, commend, extol their graces;<br>Though ne’er so black, say they have angels’ faces.<br>That man that hath a tongue I say is no man<br>If with his tongue he cannot win a woman. |
| DUKE | But she I mean is promised by her friends<br>Unto a youthful gentleman of worth,<br>And kept severely from resort of men,<br>That no man hath access by day to her. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Why then I would resort to her by [[Night\|night]]. |
| DUKE | Ay, but the doors be locked and keys kept safe,<br>That no man hath recourse to her by [[Night\|night]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | What lets but one may enter at her [[Window\|window]]? |
| DUKE | Her chamber is aloft, far from the ground,<br>And built so shelving that one cannot climb it<br>Without apparent hazard of his life. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Why then, a ladder quaintly made of cords<br>To cast up, with a pair of anchoring hooks,<br>Would serve to scale another [[Hero]]’s tower,<br>So bold Leander would adventure it. |
| DUKE | Now as thou art a gentleman of [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Advise me where I may have such a ladder. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | When would you use it? Pray sir, tell me that. |
| DUKE | This very [[Night\|night]]; for [[Love\|love]] is like a [[Child\|child]]<br>That longs for everything that he can come by. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | By seven o’[[Clock\|clock]] I’ll get you such a ladder. |
| DUKE | But hark thee: I will go to her [[Alone\|alone]].<br>How shall I best convey the ladder thither? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | It will be light, my lord, that you may bear it<br>Under a cloak that is of any length. |
| DUKE | A cloak as long as thine will serve the turn? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Ay, my good lord. |
| DUKE | Then let me see thy cloak,<br>I’ll get me one of such another length. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Why, any cloak will serve the turn, my lord. |
| DUKE | How shall I fashion me to wear a cloak?<br>I pray thee let me feel thy cloak upon me.<br>He lifts [[Valentine]]’s cloak and finds a letter and a rope-ladder<br>What letter is this same? What’s here? ‘To [[Silvia]]’?<br>And here an engine fit for my proceeding.<br>I’ll be so bold to break the seal for once.<br>(Reads)<br>‘My thoughts do harbour with my [[Silvia]] nightly,<br>And slaves they are to me, that send them flying.<br>O, could their master come and go as lightly,<br>Himself would lodge where, senseless, they are lying.<br>My herald thoughts in thy pure bosom rest them,<br>While I, their [[King\|king]], that thither them importune,<br>Do curse the grace that with such grace hath blessed<br>them,<br>Because myself do want my servants’ fortune.<br>I curse myself for they are sent by me,<br>That they should harbour where their lord should be.’<br>What’s here?<br>‘[[Silvia]], this [[Night\|night]] I will enfranchise thee’?<br>‘Tis so, and here’s the ladder for the purpose.<br>Why, Phaeton, for thou art Merops’ son<br>Wilt thou aspire to guide the heavenly car,<br>And with thy daring folly burn the world?<br>Wilt thou reach [[Stars\|stars]] because they shine on thee?<br>Go, base intruder, over-weening slave,<br>Bestow thy fawning smiles on equal mates,<br>And think my patience, more than thy [[Desert\|desert]],<br>Is privilege for thy departure hence.<br>Thank me for this more than for all the favours<br>Which, all too much, I have bestowed on thee.<br>But if thou linger in my territories<br>Longer than swiftest expedition<br>Will give thee [[Time\|time]] to leave our royal [[Court\|court]],<br>By heaven, my wrath shall far exceed the [[Love\|love]]<br>I ever bore my daughter or thyself.<br>Be gone. I will not hear thy vain excuse,<br>But as thou lov’st thy life, make [[Speed\|speed]] from hence. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | And why not [[Death\|death]], rather than living torment?<br>To die is to be banished from myself,<br>And [[Silvia]] is my [[Self\|self]]. Banished from her<br>Is [[Self\|self]] from [[Self\|self]], a deadly banishment.<br>What light is light, if [[Silvia]] be not seen?<br>What joy is joy, if [[Silvia]] be not by—<br>Unless it be to think that she is by,<br>And feed upon the [[Shadow\|shadow]] of perfection.<br>Except I be by [[Silvia]] in the [[Night\|night]]<br>There is no music in the [[Nightingale\|nightingale]].<br>Unless I look on [[Silvia]] in the day<br>There is no day for me to look upon.<br>She is my essence, and I leave to be<br>If I be not by her fair influence<br>Fostered, illumined, cherished, kept alive.<br>I fly not [[Death\|death]] to fly his deadly doom.<br>Tarry I here I but attend on [[Death\|death]],<br>But fly I hence, I fly away from life. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Proteus]] and Lance |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Run, boy, run, run, and seek him out. |
| LANCE | So-ho, so-ho! |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | What seest thou? |
| LANCE | Him we go to find. There’s not a hair on’s head but ‘tis [[A Valentine\|a Valentine]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | [[Valentine]]? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | No. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Who then—his spirit? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Neither. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | What then? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Nothing. |
| LANCE | Can nothing speak?<br>He threatens [[Valentine]]<br>Master, shall I strike? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Who wouldst thou strike? |
| LANCE | Nothing. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | [[Villain]], forbear. |
| LANCE | Why, sir, I’ll strike nothing. I pray you— |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Sirrah, I say forbear. Friend [[Valentine]], a word. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | My ears are stopped, and cannot hear good news,<br>So much of bad already hath possessed them. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Then in dumb [[Silence\|silence]] will I bury mine,<br>For they are harsh, untuneable, and bad. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Is [[Silvia]] dead? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | No, [[Valentine]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | No [[Valentine]] indeed, for sacred [[Silvia]].<br>Hath she forsworn me? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | No, [[Valentine]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | No [[Valentine]], if [[Silvia]] have forsworn me.<br>What is your news? |
| LANCE | Sir, there is a proclamation that you are vanished. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | That thou art banished. O that’s the news:<br>From hence, from [[Silvia]], and from me thy friend. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | O, I have fed upon this woe already,<br>And now excess of it will make me surfeit.<br>Doth [[Silvia]] know that I am banishèd? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Ay, ay; and she hath offered to the doom,<br>Which unreversed stands in effectual force,<br>A sea of melting pearl, which some call [[Tears\|tears]].<br>Those at her father’s churlish feet she tendered,<br>With them, upon her knees, her humble [[Self\|self]],<br>Wringing her hands, whose whiteness so became them<br>As if but now they waxed pale, for woe.<br>But neither bended knees, pure hands held up,<br>Sad sighs, deep groans, nor silver-shedding [[Tears\|tears]]<br>Could penetrate her uncompassionate sire,<br>But [[Valentine]], if he be ta’en, must die.<br>Besides, her intercession chafed him so<br>When she for thy repeal was suppliant<br>That to close [[Prison\|prison]] he commanded her,<br>With many bitter threats of biding there. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | No more, unless the next word that thou speak’st<br>Have some malignant power upon my life.<br>If so I pray thee breathe it in mine ear,<br>As ending anthem of my endless dolour. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Cease to lament for that thou canst not help,<br>And study help for that which thou lament‘st.<br>[[Time]] is the [[Nurse\|nurse]] and breeder of all good.<br>Here if thou stay thou canst not see thy [[Love\|love]].<br>Besides, thy staying will abridge thy life.<br>[[Hope]] is a [[Lover\|lover]]’s staff. Walk hence with that,<br>And manage it against despairing thoughts.<br>Thy letters may be here, though thou art hence,<br>Which, being writ to me, shall be delivered<br>Even in the milk-white bosom of thy [[Love\|love]].<br>The [[Time\|time]] now serves not to expostulate.<br>Come, I’ll convey thee through the [[City\|city]] gate,<br>And ere I part with thee confer at large<br>Of all that may concern thy [[Love\|love]] affairs.<br>As thou lov’st [[Silvia]], though not for thyself,<br>Regard thy danger, and along with me. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I pray thee, Lance, an if thou seest my boy<br>Bid him make haste, and meet me at the North Gate. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Go, sirrah, find him out. Come, [[Valentine]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | O my dear [[Silvia]]! Hapless [[Valentine]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Proteus]] and [[Valentine]] |
| LANCE | I am but a [[Fool\|fool]], look you, and yet I have the wit to think my master is a kind of a knave. But that’s all one, if he be but one knave. He lives not now that knows me to be in [[Love\|love]], yet I am in [[Love\|love]], but a team of [[Horse\|horse]] shall not pluck that from me, nor who ‘tis I [[Love\|love]]; and yet ’tis a woman, but what woman I will not tell myself; and yet ‘tis a milkmaid; yet ‘tis not a maid, for she hath had gossips; yet ’tis a maid, for she is her master’s maid, and serves for wages. She hath more qualities than a [[Water\|water]]-spaniel, which is much in a bare Christian.<br>He takes out a paper<br>Here is the catalogue of her conditions. ‘Imprimis, she can fetch and carry’—why, a [[Horse\|horse]] can do no more. Nay, a [[Horse\|horse]] cannot fetch, but only carry, therefore is she better than a jade. ‘Item, she can milk.’ Look you, a sweet virtue in a maid with clean hands. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Speed]] |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | How now, Signor Lance, what news with your mastership? |
| LANCE | With my master’s [[Ship\|ship]]? Why, it is at sea. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | [[Well]], your old vice still, mistake the word. What news then in your paper? |
| LANCE | The blackest news that ever thou heard’st. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why, man, how ‘black’? |
| LANCE | Why, as black as ink. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Let me read them. |
| LANCE | Fie on thee, jolt-head, thou canst not read. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Thou liest. I can. |
| LANCE | I will try thee. Tell me this: who begot thee? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Marry, the son of my grandfather. |
| LANCE | O illiterate loiterer, it was the son of thy grand-mother. This proves that thou canst not read. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Come, [[Fool\|fool]], come. Try me in thy paper. |
| LANCE | (giving [[Speed]] the paper) There: and Saint Nicholas be thy [[Speed\|speed]]. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Imprimis, she can milk.’ |
| LANCE | Ay, that she can. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she brews good ale.’ |
| LANCE | And thereof comes the proverb ‘Blessing of your heart, you brew good ale’. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she can sew.’ |
| LANCE | That’s as much as to say ‘Can she so?’ |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she can knit.’ |
| LANCE | What need a man care for a stock with a wench when she can knit him a stock? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she can wash and scour.’ |
| LANCE | A special virtue, for then she need not be washed and scoured. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she can spin.’ |
| LANCE | Then may I set the world on wheels, when she can spin for her living. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she hath many nameless virtues.’ |
| LANCE | That’s as much as to say ‘bastard virtues’, that indeed know not their fathers, and therefore have no names. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Here follows her vices. |
| LANCE | Close at the heels of her virtues. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she is not to be broken with fasting, in respect of her breath.’ |
