| LANCE | [[Well]], that fault may be mended with a breakfast. Read on. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she hath a sweet mouth.’ |
| LANCE | That makes amends for her sour breath. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she doth talk in her [[Sleep\|sleep]].’ |
| LANCE | It’s no matter for that, so she [[Sleep\|sleep]] not in her talk. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she is slow in words.’ |
| LANCE | O [[Villain\|villain]], that set this down among her vices! To be slow in words is a woman’s only virtue. I pray thee out with’t, and place it for her chief virtue. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she is proud.’ |
| LANCE | Out with that, too. It was Eve’s legacy, and cannot be ta’en from her. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she hath no teeth.’ |
| LANCE | I care not for that, neither, because I [[Love\|love]] crusts. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she is curst.’ |
| LANCE | [[Well]], the best is, she hath no teeth to bite. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she will often praise her liquor.’ |
| LANCE | If her liquor be good, she shall. If she will not, I will; for good things should be praised. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she is too liberal.’ |
| LANCE | Of her tongue she cannot, for that’s writ down she is slow of. Of her purse she shall not, for that I’ll keep shut. Now of another thing she may, and that cannot I help. [[Well]], proceed. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she hath more hair than wit, and more faults than hairs, and more wealth than faults.’ |
| LANCE | Stop there. I’ll have her. She was mine and not mine twice or thrice in that last article. Rehearse that once more. 347 |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘Item, she hath more hair than wit’— |
| LANCE | ‘More hair than wit.’ It may be. I’ll prove it: the cover of the salt hides the salt, and therefore it is more than the salt. The hair that covers the wit is more than the wit, for the greater hides the less. What’s next? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘And more faults than hairs’— |
| LANCE | That’s monstrous. O that that were out! |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | ‘And more wealth than faults.’ |
| LANCE | Why, that word makes the faults gracious. [[Well]], I’ll have her, and if it be a match—as nothing is impossible— |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | What then? |
| LANCE | Why then will I tell thee that thy master stays for thee at the North Gate. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | For me? |
| LANCE | For thee? Ay, who art thou? He hath stayed for a better man than thee. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | And must I go to him? |
| LANCE | Thou must run to him, for thou hast stayed so long that going will scarce serve the turn. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why didst not tell me sooner? Pox of your [[Love\|love]] letters! Exit |
| LANCE | Now will he be swinged for reading my letter. An unmannerly slave, that will thrust himself into [[Secrets\|secrets]]. I’ll after, to rejoice in the boy’s correction. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |

### Atto 3, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Duke and [[Thurio]] |
| DUKE | Sir [[Thurio]], [[Fear\|fear]] not but that she will [[Love\|love]] you<br>Now [[Valentine]] is banished from her sight. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Since his [[Exile\|exile]] she hath despised me most,<br>Forsworn my company, and railed at me,<br>That I am desperate of obtaining her. |
| DUKE | This weak impress of [[Love\|love]] is as a figure<br>Trenched in ice, which with an hour’s heat<br>Dissolves to [[Water\|water]] and doth lose his form.<br>A little [[Time\|time]] will melt her frozen thoughts,<br>And worthless [[Valentine]] shall be forgot. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Proteus]] |
| DUKE | How now, Sir [[Proteus]], is your countryman,<br>According to our proclamation, gone? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Gone, my good lord. |
| DUKE | My daughter takes his going grievously? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | A little [[Time\|time]], my lord, will kill that grief. |
| DUKE | So I believe, but [[Thurio]] thinks not so.<br>[[Proteus]], the good conceit I hold of thee—<br>For thou hast shown some sign of good [[Desert\|desert]]—<br>Makes me the better to confer with thee. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Longer than I prove loyal to your grace<br>Let me not live to look upon your grace. |
| DUKE | Thou know’st how willingly I would effect<br>The match between Sir [[Thurio]] and my daughter? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I do, my lord. |
| DUKE | And also, I think, thou art not ignorant<br>How she opposes her against my will? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | She did, my lord, when [[Valentine]] was here. |
| DUKE | Ay, and perversely she persevers so.<br>What might we do to make the girl forget<br>The [[Love\|love]] of [[Valentine]], and [[Love\|love]] Sir [[Thurio]]? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | The best way is to slander [[Valentine]]<br>With falsehood, cowardice, and poor descent,<br>Three things that women highly hold in hate. |
| DUKE | Ay, but she’ll think that it is spoke in hate. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Ay, if his enemy deliver it.<br>Therefore it must with circumstance be spoken<br>By one whom she esteemeth as his friend. |
| DUKE | Then you must undertake to slander him. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | And that, my lord, I shall be loath to do.<br>’Tis an ill office for a gentleman,<br>Especially against his very friend. |
| DUKE | Where your good word cannot advantage him<br>Your slander never can endamage him.<br>Therefore the office is indifferent,<br>Being entreated to it by your friend. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | You have prevailed, my lord. If I can do it<br>By aught that I can speak in his dispraise<br>She shall not long continue [[Love\|love]] to him.<br>But say this weed her [[Love\|love]] from [[Valentine]],<br>It follows not that she will [[Love\|love]] Sir [[Thurio]]. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Therefore, as you unwind her [[Love\|love]] from him,<br>Lest it should ravel and be good to none<br>You must provide to [[Bottom\|bottom]] it on me;<br>Which must be done by praising me as much<br>As you in worth dispraise Sir [[Valentine]]. |
| DUKE | And [[Proteus]], we dare trust you in this kind<br>Because we know, on [[Valentine]]’s report,<br>You are already [[Love\|love]]’s firm votary,<br>And cannot soon revolt, and change your mind.<br>Upon this warrant shall you have access<br>Where you with [[Silvia]] may confer at large.<br>For she is lumpish, heavy, [[Melancholy\|melancholy]],<br>And for your friend’s sake will be glad of you;<br>Where you may temper her, by your [[Persuasion\|persuasion]],<br>To hate young [[Valentine]] and [[Love\|love]] my friend. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | As much as I can do, I will effect.<br>But you, Sir [[Thurio]], are not sharp enough.<br>You must lay lime to tangle her desires<br>By wailful sonnets, whose composed rhymes<br>Should be full-fraught with serviceable vows. |
| DUKE | Ay, much is the force of heaven-bred poesy. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Say that upon the altar of her [[Beauty\|beauty]]<br>You sacrifice your [[Tears\|tears]], your sighs, your heart.<br>Write till your ink be dry, and with your [[Tears\|tears]]<br>Moist it again; and frame some feeling line<br>That may discover such integrity;<br>For Orpheus’ lute was strung with poets’ sinews,<br>Whose golden touch could soften steel and stones,<br>Make tigers tame, and huge leviathans<br>Forsake unsounded deeps to [[Dance\|dance]] on sands.<br>After your dire-lamenting elegies,<br>Visit by [[Night\|night]] your lady’s chamber-[[Window\|window]]<br>With some sweet consort. To their instruments<br>Tune a deploring dump. The [[Night\|night]]’s dead [[Silence\|silence]]<br>Will [[Well\|well]] become such sweet-complaining grievance.<br>This, or else nothing, will inherit her. |
| DUKE | This discipline shows thou hast been in [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | And thy advice this [[Night\|night]] I’ll put in practice.<br>Therefore, sweet [[Proteus]], my direction-giver,<br>Let us into the [[City\|city]] presently<br>To sort some gentlemen [[Well\|well]] skilled in music.<br>I have a [[Sonnet\|sonnet]] that will serve the turn<br>To give the onset to thy good advice. |
| DUKE | About it, gentlemen. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | We’ll wait upon your grace till after supper,<br>And afterward determine our proceedings. |
| DUKE | Even now about it. I will pardon you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Thurio]] and [[Proteus]] at one door, and the Duke at another |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Outlaws |
| FIRST OUTLAW | Fellows, stand fast. I see a passenger. |
| SECOND OUTLAW | If there be ten, shrink not, but down with ‘em. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Valentine]] and [[Speed]] |
| THIRD OUTLAW | Stand, sir, and throw us that you have about ye.<br>If not, we’ll make you sit, and rifle you. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (to [[Valentine]])<br>Sir, we are undone. These are the villains<br>That all the travellers do [[Fear\|fear]] so much. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | (to the Outlaws) My friends. |
| FIRST OUTLAW | That’s not so, sir. We are your enemies. |
| SECOND OUTLAW | Peace. We’ll hear him. |
| THIRD OUTLAW | Ay, by my beard will we. For he is a proper man. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Then know that I have little wealth to lose.<br>A man I am, crossed with adversity.<br>My riches are these poor habiliments,<br>Of which if you should here disfurnish me<br>You take the sum and substance that I have. |
| SECOND OUTLAW | Whither travel you? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | To [[Verona]]. |
| FIRST OUTLAW | Whence came you? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | From Milan. 20 |
| THIRD OUTLAW | Have you long sojourned there? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Some sixteen months, and longer might have stayed<br>If crooked fortune had not thwarted me. |
| FIRST OUTLAW | What, were you banished thence? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I was. |
| SECOND OUTLAW | For what offence? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | For that which now torments me to rehearse.<br>I killed a man, whose [[Death\|death]] I much repent,<br>But yet I slew him manfully, in fight,<br>Without false vantage or base treachery. |
| FIRST OUTLAW | Why, ne’er repent it, if it were done so.<br>But were you banished for so small a fault? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I was, and held me glad of such a doom. |
| SECOND OUTLAW | Have you the tongues? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | My youthful travel therein made me happy,<br>Or else I had been often miserable. |
| THIRD OUTLAW | By the bare scalp of [[Robin Hood]]’s fat friar,<br>This fellow were a [[King\|king]] for our wild faction. |
| FIRST OUTLAW | We’ll have him. Sirs, a word.<br>The Outlaws confer |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (to [[Valentine]]) Master, be one of them.<br>It’s an honourable kind of thievery. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Peace, [[Villain\|villain]]. |
| SECOND OUTLAW | Tell us this: have you anything to take to? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Nothing but my fortune. |
| THIRD OUTLAW | Know, then, that some of us are gentlemen<br>Such as the fury of ungoverned youth<br>Thrust from the company of aweful men.<br>Myself was from [[Verona]] banished<br>For practising to steal away a lady,<br>An heir, and near allied unto the Duke. |
| SECOND OUTLAW | And I from Mantua, for a gentleman<br>Who, in my mood, I stabbed unto the heart. |
| FIRST OUTLAW | And I, for suchlike petty crimes as these.<br>But to the purpose, for we cite our faults<br>That they may hold excused our lawless lives.<br>And partly seeing you are beautified<br>With goodly shape, and by your own report<br>A linguist, and a man of such perfection<br>As we do in our quality much want— |
| SECOND OUTLAW | Indeed because you are a banished man,<br>Therefore above the rest we parley to you.<br>Are you content to be our general,<br>To make a virtue of necessity<br>And live as we do in this wilderness? |
| THIRD OUTLAW | What sayst thou? Wilt thou be of our consort?<br>Say ‘Ay’, and be the captain of us all.<br>We’ll do thee homage, and be ruled by thee,<br>[[Love]] thee as our commander and our [[King\|king]]. |
| FIRST OUTLAW | But if thou scorn our courtesy, thou diest. |
| SECOND OUTLAW | Thou shalt not live to brag what we have offered. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I take your offer, and will live with you,<br>Provided that you do no outrages<br>On silly women or poor passengers. |
| THIRD OUTLAW | No, we detest such vile, base practices.<br>Come, go with us. We’ll bring thee to our crews<br>And show thee all the treasure we have got,<br>Which, with ourselves, all rest at thy dispose. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Proteus]] |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Already have I been false to [[Valentine]],<br>And now I must be as unjust to [[Thurio]].<br>Under the colour of commending him<br>I have access my own [[Love\|love]] to prefer.<br>But [[Silvia]] is too fair, too true, too holy<br>To be corrupted with my worthless gifts.<br>When I protest true loyalty to her<br>She twits me with my falsehood to my friend.<br>When to her [[Beauty\|beauty]] I commend my vows<br>She bids me think how I have been forsworn<br>In breaking [[Faith\|faith]] with [[Julia]], whom I loved.<br>And notwithstanding all her sudden quips,<br>The least whereof would quell a [[Lover\|lover]]’s [[Hope\|hope]],<br>Yet, spaniel-like, the more she spurns my [[Love\|love]],<br>The more it grows and fawneth on her still.<br>But here comes [[Thurio]]. Now must we to her [[Window\|window]],<br>And give some evening music to her ear. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Thurio]] with Musicians |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | How now, Sir [[Proteus]], are you crept before us? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Ay, gentle [[Thurio]], for you know that [[Love\|love]]<br>Will creep in service where it cannot go. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Ay, but I [[Hope\|hope]], sir, that you [[Love\|love]] not here. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Sir, but I do, or else I would be hence. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Who, [[Silvia]]? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Ay, [[Silvia]]—for your sake. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | I thank you for your own. Now, gentlemen,<br>Let’s tune, and to it lustily awhile. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Host, and [[Julia]], dressed as a page-boy. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | They talk apart |
| HOST | Now, my young guest, methinks you’re allycholly. I pray you, why is it? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Marry, mine host, because I cannot be merry. |
| HOST | Come, we’ll have you merry. I’ll bring you where you shall hear music, and see the gentleman that you asked for. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | But shall I hear him speak? |
| HOST | Ay, that you shall. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | That will be music. |
| HOST | Hark, hark. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Is he among these? |
| HOST | Ay. But peace, let’s hear ’em. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Song]] |
| HOST | Who is [[Silvia]]? What is she,<br>That all our swains commend her?<br>Holy, fair, and wise is she.<br>The heaven such grace did lend her<br>That she might admired be.<br>Is she kind as she is fair?<br>For [[Beauty\|beauty]] lives with kindness.<br>[[Love]] doth to her eyes repair<br>To help him of his blindness,<br>And, being helped, inhabits there.<br>Then to [[Silvia]] let us sing<br>That [[Silvia]] is excelling.<br>She excels each mortal thing<br>Upon the dull earth dwelling.<br>To her let us garlands bring. |
| HOST | How now, are you sadder than you were before? How do you, man? The music likes you not. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | You mistake. The musician likes me not. |
| HOST | Why, my pretty youth? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | He plays false, father. |
| HOST | How, out of tune on the strings? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Not so, but yet so false that he grieves my very heart-strings. |
| HOST | You have a quick ear. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Ay, I would I were deaf. It makes me have a slow heart. |
| HOST | I perceive you delight not in music. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Not a whit when it jars so. |
| HOST | Hark what fine change is in the music. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Ay, that ‘change’ is the spite. |
| HOST | You would have them always play but one thing? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | I would always have one play but one thing. But host, doth this Sir [[Proteus]] that we talk on often resort unto this gentlewoman? |
| HOST | I tell you what Lance his man told me, he loved her out of all nick. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Where is Lance? |
| HOST | Gone to seek his dog, which tomorrow, by his master’s command, he must carry for a present to his lady. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Peace, stand aside. The company parts. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Sir [[Thurio]], [[Fear\|fear]] not you. I will so plead<br>That you shall say my cunning drift excels. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Where meet we? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | At Saint Gregory’s [[Well\|well]]. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Farewell. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Thurio]] and the Musicians |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Silvia]], above |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Madam, good even to your ladyship. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | I thank you for your music, gentlemen.<br>Who is that that spake? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | One, lady, if you knew his pure heart’s [[Truth\|truth]]<br>You would quickly learn to know him by his voice. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Sir [[Proteus]], as I take it. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Sir [[Proteus]], gentle lady, and your servant. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | What’s your will? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | That I may compass yours. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | You have your wish. My will is even this,<br>That presently you hie you [[Home\|home]] to bed.<br>Thou subtle, perjured, false, disloyal man,<br>Think’st thou I am so shallow, so conceitless<br>To be seduced by thy flattery,<br>That hast deceived so many with thy vows?<br>Return, return, and make thy [[Love\|love]] amends.<br>For me—by this pale [[Queen\|queen]] of [[Night\|night]] I swear—<br>I am so far from granting thy request<br>That I despise thee for thy wrongful suit,<br>And by and by intend to chide myself<br>Even for this [[Time\|time]] I spend in talking to thee. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I grant, sweet [[Love\|love]], that I did [[Love\|love]] a lady,<br>But she is dead. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside) ‘Twere false if I should speak it,<br>For I am sure she is not buried. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Say that she be, yet [[Valentine]], thy friend,<br>Survives, to whom, thyself art witness,<br>I am betrothed. And art thou not ashamed<br>To wrong him with thy importunacy? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I likewise hear that [[Valentine]] is dead. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | And so suppose am I, for in his [[Grave\|grave]],<br>Assure thyself, my [[Love\|love]] is buried. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Sweet lady, let me rake it from the earth. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Go to thy lady’s [[Grave\|grave]] and call hers thence,<br>Or at the least, in hers sepulchre thine. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside) He heard not that. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Madam, if your heart be so obdurate,<br>Vouchsafe me yet your picture for my [[Love\|love]],<br>The picture that is hanging in your chamber.<br>To that I’ll speak, to that I’ll sigh and weep;<br>For since the substance of your perfect [[Self\|self]]<br>Is else devoted, I am but a [[Shadow\|shadow]],<br>And to your [[Shadow\|shadow]] will I make true [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside)<br>If ’twere a substance, you would sure deceive it<br>And make it but a [[Shadow\|shadow]], as I am. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | I am very loath to be your idol, sir,<br>But since your falsehood shall become you [[Well\|well]]<br>To worship shadows and adore false shapes,<br>Send to me in the morning, and I’ll send it.<br>And so, good rest. Exit |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | As wretches have o’ernight,<br>That wait for execution in the morn. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Host, will you go? |
| HOST | By my halidom, I was fast asleep. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Pray you, where lies Sir [[Proteus]]? |
| HOST | Marry, at my [[house]]. Trust me, I think ’tis almost day. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Not so; but it hath been the longest [[Night\|night]]<br>That e’er I watched, and the most heaviest. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir Eglamour |
| EGLAMOUR | This is the hour that Madam [[Silvia]]<br>Entreated me to call, and know her mind.<br>There’s some great matter she’d employ me in.<br>Madam, madam! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Silvia]] [above] |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Who calls? |
| EGLAMOUR | Your servant, and your friend. One that attends your ladyship’s command. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Sir Eglamour, a thousand times good morrow! |
| EGLAMOUR | As many, worthy lady, to yourself.<br>According to your ladyship’s impose<br>I am thus early come, to know what service<br>It is your pleasure to command me in. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | O Eglamour, thou art a gentleman—<br>Think not I flatter, for I swear I do not—<br>Valiant, wise, remorseful, [[Well\|well]] accomplished.<br>Thou art not ignorant what dear good will<br>I bear unto the banished [[Valentine]],<br>Nor how my father would enforce me marry<br>Vain [[Thurio]], whom my very [[Soul\|soul]] abhors.<br>Thyself hast loved, and I have heard thee say<br>No grief did ever come so near thy heart<br>As when thy lady and thy true [[Love\|love]] died,<br>Upon whose [[Grave\|grave]] thou vowed’st pure chastity.<br>Sir Eglamour, I would to [[Valentine]],<br>To Mantua, where I hear he makes abode;<br>And for the ways are dangerous to pass<br>I do [[Desire\|desire]] thy worthy company,<br>Upon whose [[Faith\|faith]] and [[Honour\|honour]] I repose.<br>Urge not my father’s anger, Eglamour,<br>But think upon my grief, a lady’s grief,<br>And on the justice of my flying hence<br>To keep me from a most unholy match,<br>Which heaven and fortune still rewards with plagues.<br>I do [[Desire\|desire]] thee, even from a heart<br>As full of sorrows as the sea of sands,<br>To bear me company and go with me.<br>If not, to hide what I have said to thee<br>That I may venture to depart [[Alone\|alone]]. |
| EGLAMOUR | Madam, I pity much your grievances,<br>Which, since I know they virtuously are placed,<br>I give consent to go along with you,<br>Recking as little what betideth me<br>As much I wish all good befortune you.<br>When will you go? |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | This evening coming. |
| EGLAMOUR | Where shall I meet you? |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | At Friar Patrick’s cell,<br>Where I intend holy confession. |
| EGLAMOUR | I will not fail your ladyship.<br>Good morrow, gentle lady. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Good morrow, kind Sir Eglamour. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lance and his dog Crab |
| LANCE | (to the audience) When a man’s servant shall play the cur with him, look you, it goes hard. One that I brought up of a puppy, one that I saved from drowning when three or four of his blind brothers and sisters went to it. I have taught him, even as one would say precisely ‘Thus I would teach a dog’. I was sent to deliver him as a present to Mistress [[Silvia]] from my master, and I came no sooner into the dining-chamber but he steps me to her trencher and steals her capon’s leg. O, ‘tis a foul thing when a cur cannot keep himself in all companies. I would have, as one should say, one that takes upon him to be a dog indeed, to be, as it were, a dog at all things. If I had not had more wit than he, to take a fault upon me that he did, I think verily he had been hanged for’t. Sure as I live, he had suffered for’t. You shall judge. He thrusts me himself into the company of three or four gentleman-like [[dogs]] under the Duke’s table. He had not been there—bless the mark—a pissing-while but all the chamber smelled him. ‘Out with the dog,’ says one. ‘What cur is that?’ says another. ‘Whip him out,’ says the third. ‘Hang him up,’ says the Duke. I, having been acquainted with the smell before, knew it was Crab, and goes me to the fellow that whips the [[dogs]]. ‘Friend,’ quoth I, ‘you mean to whip the dog.’ ‘Ay, marry do I,’ quoth he. ‘You do him the more wrong,’ quoth I, “twas I did the thing you wot of.’ He makes me no more ado, but whips me out of the chamber. How many masters would do this for his servant? Nay, I’ll be sworn I have sat in the stocks for puddings he hath stolen, otherwise he had been executed. I have stood on the pillory for geese he hath killed, otherwise he had suffered for’t. (To Crab) Thou think’st not of this now. Nay, I remember the trick you served me when I took my leave of Madam [[Silvia]]. Did not I bid thee still mark me, and do as I do? When didst thou see me heave up my leg and make [[Water\|water]] against a gentlewoman’s farthingale? Didst thou ever see me do such a trick? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Proteus]], with [[Julia]] dressed as a page-boy |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | (to [[Julia]])<br>[[Sebastian]] is thy name? I like thee [[Well\|well]],<br>And will employ thee in some service presently. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | In what you please. I’ll do what I can. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I [[Hope\|hope]] thou wilt.—How now, you whoreson peasant,<br>Where have you been these two days loitering? |
| LANCE | Marry, sir, I carried Mistress [[Silvia]] the dog you bade me. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | And what says she to my little [[Jewel\|jewel]]? |
| LANCE | Marry, she says your dog was a cur, and tells you currish thanks is good enough for such a present. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But she received my dog? |
| LANCE | No indeed did she not. Here have I brought him back again. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | What, didst thou offer her this from me? |
| LANCE | Ay, sir. The other squirrel was stolen from me by the hangman boys in the market place, and then I offered her mine own, who is a dog as big as ten of yours, and therefore the gift the greater. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Go, get thee hence, and find my dog again,<br>Or ne‘er return again into my sight.<br>Away, I say. Stay’st thou to vex me here? |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Lance with Crab |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | A slave, that still on end turns me to shame.<br>[[Sebastian]], I have entertained thee<br>Partly that I have need of such a youth<br>That can with some discretion do my business,<br>For ’tis no trusting to yon foolish lout,<br>But chiefly for thy face and thy behaviour,<br>Which, if my augury deceive me not,<br>Witness good bringing up, fortune, and [[Truth\|truth]].<br>Therefore know thou, for this I entertain thee.<br>Go presently, and take this ring with thee.<br>Deliver it to Madam [[Silvia]].<br>She loved me [[Well\|well]] delivered it to me. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | It seems you loved not her, to leave her token.<br>She is dead belike? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Not so. I think she lives. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Alas.<br>[[Proteus]] Why dost thou cry ‘Alas’? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | I cannot choose but pity her. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Wherefore shouldst thou pity her? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Because methinks that she loved you as [[Well\|well]]<br>As you do [[Love\|love]] your lady [[Silvia]].<br>She [[Dreams\|dreams]] on him that has forgot her [[Love\|love]];<br>You dote on her that cares not for your [[Love\|love]].<br>‘Tis pity [[Love\|love]] should be so contrary,<br>And thinking on it makes me cry ‘Alas’. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | [[Well]], give her that ring, and therewithal<br>This letter. (Pointing) That’s her chamber. Tell my<br>lady<br>I claim the promise for her heavenly picture.<br>Your message done, hie [[Home\|home]] unto my chamber,<br>Where thou shalt find me sad and solitary. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | How many women would do such a message?<br>Alas, poor [[Proteus]], thou hast entertained<br>A fox to be the shepherd of thy lambs.<br>Alas, poor [[Fool\|fool]], why do I pity him<br>That with his very heart despiseth me?<br>Because he loves her, he despiseth me.<br>Because I [[Love\|love]] him, I must pity him.<br>This ring I gave him when he parted from me,<br>To bind him to remember my good will.<br>And now am I, unhappy messenger,<br>To plead for that which I would not obtain;<br>To carry that which I would have refused;<br>To praise his [[Faith\|faith]], which I would have dispraised.<br>I am my master’s true-confirmèd [[Love\|love]],<br>But cannot be true servant to my master<br>Unless I prove false traitor to myself.<br>Yet will I woo for him, but yet so coldly<br>As, heaven it knows, I would not have him [[Speed\|speed]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Silvia]] |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Gentlewoman, good day. I pray you be my mean<br>To bring me where to speak with Madam [[Silvia]]. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | What would you with her, if that I be she? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | If you be she, I do entreat your patience<br>To hear me speak the message I am sent on. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | From whom? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | From my master, Sir [[Proteus]], madam. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | O, he sends you for a picture? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Ay, madam. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Ursula, bring my picture there.<br>[An attendant brings a picture]<br>Go, give your master this. Tell him from me<br>One [[Julia]], that his changing thoughts forget,<br>Would better fit his chamber than this [[Shadow\|shadow]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Madam, please you peruse this letter.<br>She gives [[Silvia]] a letter<br>Pardon me, madam, I have unadvised<br>Delivered you a paper that I should not.<br>She takes back the letter and gives [[Silvia]] another letter<br>This is the letter to your ladyship. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | I pray thee, let me look on that again. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | It may not be. Good madam, pardon me. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | There, hold. I will not look upon your master’s lines.<br>I know they are stuffed with protestations,<br>And full of new-found oaths, which he will break<br>As easily as I do tear his paper.<br>She [[Tears\|tears]] the letter |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Madam, he sends your ladyship this ring.<br>She offers [[Silvia]] a ring |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | The more shame for him, that he sends it me;<br>For I have heard him say a thousand times<br>His [[Julia]] gave it him at his departure.<br>Though his false finger have profaned the ring,<br>Mine shall not do his [[Julia]] so much wrong. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | She thanks you. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | What sayst thou? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | I thank you, madam, that you tender her.<br>Poor gentlewoman, my master wrongs her much. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Dost thou know her? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Almost as [[Well\|well]] as I do know myself.<br>To think upon her woes I do protest<br>That I have wept a hundred several times. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Belike she thinks that [[Proteus]] hath forsook her? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | I think she doth; and that’s her cause of sorrow. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Is she not passing fair? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | She hath been fairer, madam, than she is.<br>When she did think my master loved her [[Well\|well]]<br>She, in my judgement, was as fair as you.<br>But since she did neglect her looking-glass,<br>And threw her sun-expelling mask away,<br>The air hath starved the roses in her cheeks<br>And pinched the lily tincture of her face,<br>That now she is become as black as I. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | How tall was she? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | About my stature; for at Pentecost,<br>When all our pageants of delight were played,<br>Our youth got me to play the woman’s part,<br>And I was trimmed in Madam [[Julia]]’s gown,<br>Which served me as fit, by all men’s judgements,<br>As if the garment had been made for me;<br>Therefore I know she is about my height.<br>And at that [[Time\|time]] I made her weep agood,<br>For I did play a lamentable part.<br>Madam, ’twas Ariadne, passioning<br>For [[Theseus]]’ perjury and unjust flight;<br>Which I so lively acted with my [[Tears\|tears]]<br>That my poor mistress, moved therewithal,<br>Wept bitterly; and would I might be dead<br>If I in thought felt not her very sorrow. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | She is beholden to thee, gentle youth.<br>Alas, poor lady, desolate and left.<br>I weep myself to think upon thy words.<br>Here, youth. There is my purse. I give thee this<br>For thy sweet mistress’ sake, because thou lov’st her.<br>Farewell. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And she shall thank you for‘t, if e’er you know her.—<br>A virtuous gentlewoman, mild, and beautiful.<br>I [[Hope\|hope]] my master’s suit will be but cold,<br>Since she respects ‘my mistress” [[Love\|love]] so much.<br>Alas, how [[Love\|love]] can trifle with itself.<br>Here is her picture. Let me see, I think<br>If I had such a tire, this face of mine<br>Were full as lovely as is this of hers.<br>And yet the painter flattered her a little,<br>Unless I flatter with myself too much.<br>Her hair is auburn, mine is perfect yellow.<br>If that be all the difference in his [[Love\|love]],<br>I’ll get me such a coloured periwig.<br>Her eyes are grey as glass, and so are mine.<br>Ay, but her forehead’s low, and mine’s as high.<br>What should it be that he respects in her<br>But I can make respective in myself,<br>If this fond [[Love\|love]] were not a blinded god?<br>Come, [[Shadow\|shadow]], come, and take this [[Shadow\|shadow]] up,<br>For ’tis thy rival.<br>She picks up the portrait<br>O thou senseless form,<br>Thou shalt be worshipped, kissed, loved, and adored;<br>And were there sense in his idolatry<br>My substance should be statue in thy stead.<br>I’ll use thee kindly, for thy mistress’ sake,<br>That used me so; or else, by Jove I vow,<br>I should have scratched out your unseeing eyes,<br>To make my master out of [[Love\|love]] with thee. Exit |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir Eglamour |
| EGLAMOUR | The sun begins to gild the western sky,<br>And now it is about the very hour<br>That [[Silvia]] at Friar Patrick’s cell should meet me.<br>She will not fail; for [[lovers]] break not hours,<br>Unless it be to come before their [[Time\|time]],<br>So much they spur their expedition. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Silvia]] |
| EGLAMOUR | See where she comes. Lady, a happy evening! |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Amen, amen. Go on, good Eglamour,<br>Out at the postern by the abbey wall.<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] I am attended by some spies. |
| EGLAMOUR | [[Fear]] not. The forest is not three leagues off.<br>If we recover that, we are sure enough. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Thurio]], [[Proteus]], and [[Julia]] dressed as a pageboy |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Sir [[Proteus]], what says [[Silvia]] to my suit? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | O sir, I find her milder than she was,<br>And yet she takes exceptions at your person. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | What? That my leg is too long? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | No, that it is too little. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | I’ll wear a boot, to make it somewhat rounder. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside)<br>But [[Love\|love]] will not be spurred to what it loathes. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | What says she to my face? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | She says it is a fair one. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Nay, then, the wanton lies. My face is black. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But pearls are fair; and the old saying is,<br>‘Black men are pearls in beauteous ladies’ eyes’. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside)<br>’Tis true, such pearls as put out ladies’ eyes,<br>For I had rather wink than look on them. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | How likes she my discourse? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Ill, when you talk of war. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | But [[Well\|well]] when I discourse of [[Love\|love]] and peace. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside)<br>But better indeed when you hold your peace. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | What says she to my valour? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | O sir, she makes no [[Doubt\|doubt]] of that. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside)<br>She needs not, when she knows it cowardice. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | What says she to my birth? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | That you are [[Well\|well]] derived. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside)<br>True: from a gentleman to a [[Fool\|fool]]. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Considers she my possessions? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | O ay, and pities them. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Wherefore? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside)<br>That such an ass should owe them. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | That they are out by lease. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Here comes the Duke. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Duke |
| DUKE | How now, Sir [[Proteus]]. How now, [[Thurio]].<br>Which of you saw Eglamour of late? |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Not I. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Nor I. |
| DUKE | Saw you my daughter? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Neither. |
| DUKE | Why then, she’s fled unto that peasant [[Valentine]],<br>And Eglamour is in her company.<br>’Tis true, for [[Friar Laurence]] met them both<br>As he in penance wandered through the forest.<br>Him he knew [[Well\|well]], and guessed that it was she,<br>But being masked, he was not sure of it.<br>Besides, she did intend confession<br>At Patrick’s cell this even, and there she was not.<br>These likelihoods confirm her flight from hence;<br>Therefore I pray you stand not to discourse,<br>But mount you presently, and meet with me<br>Upon the rising of the mountain foot<br>That leads toward Mantua, whither they are fled.<br>Dispatch, sweet gentlemen, and follow me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Why, this it is to be a peevish girl,<br>That flies her fortune when it follows her.<br>I’ll after, more to be revenged on Eglamour<br>Than for the [[Love\|love]] of reckless [[Silvia]].<br>[exit] |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | And I will follow, more for [[Silvia]]’s [[Love\|love]]<br>Than hate of Eglamour that goes with her.<br>[Exit] |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And I will follow, more to [[Cross\|cross]] that [[Love\|love]]<br>Than hate for [[Silvia]], that is gone for [[Love\|love]].<br>[Exit] |

### Atto 5, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Outlaws with [[Silvia]] captive |
| FIRST OUTLAW | Come, come, be patient. We must bring you to our captain. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | A thousand more mischances than this one<br>Have learned me how to brook this patiently. |
| SECOND OUTLAW | Come, bring her away. |
| FIRST OUTLAW | Where is the gentleman that was with her? |
| THIRD OUTLAW | Being nimble-footed he hath outrun us;<br>But Moses and Valerius follow him.<br>Go thou with her to the west end of the wood.<br>There is our captain. We’ll follow him that’s fled.<br>The thicket is beset, he cannot scape. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the Second and Third Outlaws |
| FIRST OUTLAW | (to [[Silvia]])<br>Come, I must bring you to our captain’s [[Cave\|cave]].<br>[[Fear]] not. He bears an honourable mind,<br>And will not use a woman lawlessly. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | (aside)<br>O [[Valentine]]! This I endure for thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Valentine]] |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | How use doth breed a habit in a man!<br>This shadowy [[Desert\|desert]], unfrequented woods<br>I better brook than flourishing peopled towns.<br>Here can I sit [[Alone\|alone]], unseen of any,<br>And to the [[Nightingale\|nightingale]]’s complaining notes<br>Tune my distresses and record my woes.<br>O thou that dost inhabit in my breast,<br>Leave not the mansion so long tenantless<br>Lest, growing ruinous, the building fall<br>And leave no [[Memory\|memory]] of what it was.<br>Repair me with thy presence, [[Silvia]].<br>Thou gentle nymph, cherish thy forlorn swain.<br>What hallooing and what stir is this today?<br>These are my mates, that make their wills their law,<br>Have some unhappy passenger in chase.<br>They [[Love\|love]] me [[Well\|well]], yet I have much to do<br>To keep them from uncivil outrages.<br>Withdraw thee, [[Valentine]]. Who’s this comes here?<br>He stands aside. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Proteus]], [[Silvia]], and [[Julia]] dressed as a pageboy |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Madam, this service I have done for you—<br>Though you respect not aught your servant doth—<br>To hazard life, and rescue you from him<br>That would have forced your [[Honour\|honour]] and your [[Love\|love]].<br>Vouchsafe me for my meed but one fair look.<br>A smaller boon than this I cannot beg,<br>And less than this I am sure you cannot give. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | (aside)<br>How like [[A Dream\|a dream]] is this I see and hear!<br>[[Love]] lend me patience to forbear awhile. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | O miserable, unhappy that I am! |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Unhappy were you, madam, ere I came.<br>But by my coming I have made you happy. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | By thy approach thou mak’st me most unhappy. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | (aside)<br>And me, when he approacheth to your presence. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | Had I been seized by a hungry [[Lion\|lion]]<br>I would have been a breakfast to the beast<br>Rather than have false [[Proteus]] rescue me.<br>O heaven be judge how I [[Love\|love]] [[Valentine]],<br>Whose life’s as tender to me as my [[Soul\|soul]].<br>And full as much, for more there cannot be,<br>I do detest false perjured [[Proteus]].<br>Therefore be gone, solicit me no more. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | What dangerous action, stood it next to [[Death\|death]],<br>Would I not undergo for one calm look!<br>O, ’tis the curse in [[Love\|love]], and still approved,<br>When women cannot [[Love\|love]] where they’re beloved. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | When [[Proteus]] cannot [[Love\|love]] where he’s beloved.<br>Read over [[Julia]]’s heart, thy first, best [[Love\|love]],<br>For whose dear sake thou didst then rend thy [[Faith\|faith]]<br>Into a thousand oaths, and all those oaths<br>Descended into perjury to [[Love\|love]] me.<br>Thou hast no [[Faith\|faith]] left now, unless thou’dst two,<br>And that’s far worse than none. Better have none<br>Than plural [[Faith\|faith]], which is too much by one,<br>Thou counterfeit to thy true friend. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | In [[Love\|love]]<br>Who respects friend? |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | All men but [[Proteus]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Nay, if the gentle spirit of moving words<br>Can no way change you to a milder form<br>I’ll woo you like a soldier, at arm’s end,<br>And [[Love\|love]] you ’gainst the [[Nature\|nature]] of [[Love\|love]]: force ye. |
| [[Silvia\|SILVIA]] | O heaven! |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | (assailing her) I’ll force thee yield to my [[Desire\|desire]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | (coming forward)<br>Ruffian, let go that rude uncivil touch,<br>Thou friend of an ill fashion. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | [[Valentine]]! |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Thou common friend, that’s without [[Faith\|faith]] or [[Love\|love]],<br>For such is a friend now. Treacherous man,<br>Thou hast beguiled my hopes. Naught but mine eye<br>Could have persuaded me. Now I dare not say<br>I have one friend alive. Thou wouldst disprove me.<br>Who should be trusted, when one’s right hand<br>Is perjured to the bosom? [[Proteus]],<br>I am sorry I must never trust thee more,<br>But count the world a stranger for thy sake.<br>The private wound is deepest. O [[Time\|time]] most accursed,<br>’Mongst all foes that a friend should be the worst! |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | My shame and [[Guilt\|guilt]] confounds me.<br>Forgive me, [[Valentine]]. If hearty sorrow<br>Be a sufficient ransom for offence,<br>I tender’t here. I do as truly suffer<br>As e’er I did commit. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Then I am paid,<br>And once again I do receive thee honest.<br>Who by repentance is not satisfied<br>Is nor of heaven nor earth. For these are pleased;<br>By penitence th’ Eternal’s wrath’s appeased.<br>And that my [[Love\|love]] may appear plain and free,<br>All that was mine in [[Silvia]] I give thee. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | O me unhappy!<br>She faints |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Look to the boy. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Why, boy!<br>Why wag, how now? What’s the matter? Look up. Speak. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | O good sir, my master charged me to deliver a ring to Madam [[Silvia]], which out of my neglect was never done. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Where is that ring, boy? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Here ’tis. This is it.<br>She gives [[Proteus]] the ring |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | How, let me see!<br>Why, this is the ring I gave to [[Julia]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | O, cry you [[Mercy\|mercy]], sir, I have mistook.<br>She offers [[Proteus]] another ring<br>This is the ring you sent to [[Silvia]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But how cam’st thou by this ring? At my depart<br>I gave this unto [[Julia]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And [[Julia]] herself did give it me,<br>And [[Julia]] herself hath brought it hither. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | How? [[Julia]]? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Behold her that gave aim to all thy oaths<br>And entertained ’em deeply in her heart.<br>How oft hast thou with perjury cleft the root?<br>O [[Proteus]], let this habit make thee blush.<br>Be thou ashamed that I have took upon me<br>Such an immodest raiment, if shame live<br>In a disguise of [[Love\|love]].<br>It is the lesser blot, modesty finds,<br>Women to change their shapes than men their minds. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Than men their minds! ‘Tis true. O heaven, were man<br>But constant, he were perfect. That one error<br>Fills him with faults, makes him run through all th’<br>sins;<br>Inconstancy falls off ere it begins.<br>What is in [[Silvia]]’s face but I may spy<br>More fresh in [[Julia]]’s, with a constant eye? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Come, come, a hand from either.<br>Let me be blessed to make this happy close.<br>’Twere pity two such friends should be long foes.<br>[[Julia]] and [[Proteus]] join hands |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Bear witness, heaven, I have my wish for ever. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And I mine. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Outlaws with the Duke and [[Thurio]] as captives |
| OUTLAWS | A prize, a prize, a prize! |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Forbear, forbear, I say. It is my lord the Duke.<br>The Outlaws release the Duke and [[Thurio]]<br><br>(To the Duke) Your grace is welcome to a man<br>disgraced,<br>Banished [[Valentine]]. |
| DUKE | Sir [[Valentine]]! |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Yonder is [[Silvia]], and [[Silvia]]’s mine. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | [[Thurio]], give back, or else embrace thy [[Death\|death]].<br>Come not within the measure of my wrath.<br>Do not name [[Silvia]] thine. If once again,<br>[[Verona]] shall not hold thee. Here she stands.<br>Take but possession of her with a touch—<br>I dare thee but to breathe upon my [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Thurio\|THURIO]] | Sir [[Valentine]], I care not for her, I.<br>I hold him but a [[Fool\|fool]] that will endanger<br>His [[Body\|body]] for a girl that loves him not.<br>I claim her not, and therefore she is thine. |
| DUKE | The more degenerate and base art thou<br>To make such means for her as thou hast done,<br>And leave her on such slight conditions.<br>Now by the [[Honour\|honour]] of my ancestry<br>I do applaud thy spirit, [[Valentine]],<br>And think thee worthy of an empress’ [[Love\|love]].<br>Know then I here forget all former griefs,<br>Cancel all grudge, repeal thee [[Home\|home]] again,<br>Plead a new state in thy unrivalled merit,<br>To which I thus subscribe: Sir [[Valentine]],<br>Thou art a gentleman, and [[Well\|well]] derived.<br>Take thou thy [[Silvia]], for thou hast deserved her. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I thank your grace. The gift hath made me happy.<br>I now beseech you, for your daughter’s sake,<br>To grant one boon that I shall ask of you. |
| DUKE | I grant it, for thine own, whate’er it be. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | These banished men that I have kept withal<br>Are men endowed with worthy qualities.<br>Forgive them what they have committed here,<br>And let them be recalled from their [[Exile\|exile]].<br>They are reformed, civil, full of good,<br>And fit for great employment, worthy lord. |
| DUKE | Thou hast prevailed. I pardon them and thee.<br>Dispose of them as thou know’st their deserts.<br>Come, let us go. We will include all jars<br>With triumphs, mirth, and rare solemnity. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | And as we walk along I dare be bold<br>With our discourse to make your grace to smile.<br>What think you of this page, my lord? |
| DUKE | I think the boy hath grace in him. He blushes. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | I warrant you, my lord, more grace than boy. |
| DUKE | What mean you by that saying? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Please you, I’ll tell you as we pass along,<br>That you will [[Wonder\|wonder]] what hath fortunèd.<br>Come, [[Proteus]], ’tis your penance but to hear<br>The story of your loves discovered.<br>That done, our day of [[Marriage\|marriage]] shall be yours,<br>One feast, one [[house]], one mutual happiness. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_1" data-title="The Two Gentlemen of Verona — Act I, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/fool,archetype/lover,motif/chains,motif/death,motif/desire,motif/home,motif/leaves,motif/love,motif/road,motif/ship,motif/sleep,motif/time,motif/well,concept/fear,concept/honour,setting/ship,setting/verona,character/julia,character/proteus,character/speed,character/valentine"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Valentine]] and [[Proteus]] |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Cease to persuade, my loving [[Proteus]].<br>[[Home]]-keeping youth have ever homely wits.<br>Were’t not affection [[Chains\|chains]] thy tender days<br>To the sweet glances of thy honoured [[Love\|love]],<br>I rather would entreat thy company<br>To see the wonders of the world abroad<br>Than, living dully sluggardized at [[Home\|home]],<br>Wear out thy youth with shapeless idleness.<br>But since thou lov’st, [[Love\|love]] still, and thrive therein—<br>Even as I would, when I to [[Love\|love]] begin. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Wilt thou be gone? Sweet [[Valentine]], adieu.<br>Think on thy [[Proteus]] when thou haply seest<br>Some rare noteworthy object in thy travel.<br>Wish me partaker in thy happiness<br>When thou dost meet good hap; and in thy danger—<br>If ever danger do environ thee—<br>Commend thy grievance to my holy prayers;<br>For I will be thy beadsman, [[Valentine]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | And on a [[Love\|love]]-book pray for my [[success]]? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Upon some book I [[Love\|love]] I’ll pray for thee. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | That’s on some shallow story of deep [[Love\|love]]—<br>How young Leander crossed the Hellespont. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | That’s a deep story of a deeper [[Love\|love]],<br>For he was more than over-shoes in [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | ‘Tis true, for you are over-boots in [[Love\|love]],<br>And yet you never swam the Hellespont. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Over the boots? Nay, give me not the boots. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | No, I will not; for it boots thee not. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | What? |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | To be in [[Love\|love]], where scorn is bought with groans,<br>Coy looks with heart-sore sighs, one fading moment’s<br>mirth<br>With twenty watchful, weary, tedious nights.<br>If haply won, perhaps a hapless gain;<br>If lost, why then a grievous labour won;<br>However, but a folly bought with wit,<br>Or else a wit by folly vanquished. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | So by your circumstance you call me [[Fool\|fool]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | So by your circumstance I [[Fear\|fear]] you’ll prove. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | ‘Tis [[Love\|love]] you cavil at. I am not [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | [[Love]] is your master, for he masters you,<br>And he that is so yoked by a [[Fool\|fool]]<br>Methinks should not be chronicled for wise. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Yet writers say ‘As in the sweetest bud<br>The eating canker dwells, so doting [[Love\|love]]<br>Inhabits in the finest wits of all.’ |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | And writers say ‘As the most forward bud<br>Is eaten by the canker ere it blow,<br>Even so by [[Love\|love]] the young and tender wit<br>Is turned to folly, blasting in the bud,<br>Losing his verdure even in the prime,<br>And all the fair effects of future hopes.’<br>But wherefore waste I [[Time\|time]] to counsel thee<br>That art a votary to fond [[Desire\|desire]]?<br>Once more adieu. My father at the [[Road\|road]]<br>Expects my coming, there to see me shipped. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | And thither will I bring thee, [[Valentine]]. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | Sweet [[Proteus]], no. Now let us take our leave.<br>To Milan let me hear from thee by letters<br>Of thy [[success]] in [[Love\|love]], and what news else<br>Betideth here in absence of thy friend;<br>And I likewise will visit thee with mine. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | All happiness bechance to thee in Milan. |
| [[Valentine\|VALENTINE]] | As much to you at [[Home\|home]]; and so farewell. Exit |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | He after [[Honour\|honour]] hunts, I after [[Love\|love]].<br>He [[Leaves\|leaves]] his friends to dignify them more,<br>I leave myself, my friends, and all, for [[Love\|love]].<br>Thou, [[Julia]], thou hast metamorphosed me,<br>Made me neglect my studies, lose my [[Time\|time]],<br>War with good counsel, set the world at naught;<br>Made wit with musing weak, heart sick with thought. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Speed]] |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Sir [[Proteus]], save you. Saw you my master? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But now he parted hence to embark for Milan. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Twenty to one, then, he is shipped already,<br>And I have played the sheep in losing him. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Indeed, a sheep doth very often stray,<br>An if the shepherd be a while away. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | You conclude that my master is a shepherd, then,<br>and I a sheep? |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | I do. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Why then, my horns are his horns, whether I wake or [[Sleep\|sleep]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | A silly answer, and fitting [[Well\|well]] a sheep. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | This proves me still a sheep. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | True, and thy master a shepherd. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Nay, that I can deny by a circumstance. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | It shall go hard but I’ll prove it by another. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | The shepherd seeks the sheep, and not the sheep the shepherd. But I seek my master, and my master seeks not me. Therefore I am no sheep. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | The sheep for fodder follow the shepherd, the shepherd for food follows not the sheep. Thou for wages followest thy master, thy master for wages follows not thee. Therefore thou art a sheep. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Such another proof will make me cry ‘baa’. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But dost thou hear: gav’st thou my letter to [[Julia]]? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Ay, sir. I, a lost mutton, gave your letter to her, a laced mutton, and she, a laced mutton, gave me, a lost mutton, nothing for my labour. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Here’s too small a pasture for such store of muttons. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | If the ground be overcharged, you were best stick her. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Nay, in that you are astray. ‘Twere best pound you. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Nay sir, less than a pound shall serve me for carrying your letter. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | You mistake. I mean the pound, a pinfold. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | From a pound to a pin? Fold it over and over ‘Tis threefold too little for carrying a letter to your [[Lover\|lover]]. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | But what said she? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | (nods, then says) Ay. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Nod-ay? Why, that’s ‘noddy’. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | You mistook, sir. I say she did nod, and you ask me if she did nod, and I say ‘Ay’. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | And that set together is ‘noddy’. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Now you have taken the pains to set it together, take it for your pains. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | No, no. You shall have it for bearing the letter. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | [[Well]], I perceive I must be fain to bear with you. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Why, sir, how do you bear with me? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Marry, sir, the letter very orderly, having nothing but the word ‘noddy’ for my pains. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Beshrew me but you have a quick wit. |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | And yet it cannot overtake your slow purse. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Come, come, open the matter in brief. What said she? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Open your purse, that the money and the matter may be both at once delivered. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | (giving money) [[Well]], sir, here is for your pains. What said she? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Truly, sir, I think you’ll hardly win her. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Why? Couldst thou perceive so much from her? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | Sir, I could perceive nothing at all from her, no, not so much as a ducat for delivering your letter. And being so hard to me, that brought your mind, I [[Fear\|fear]] she’ll prove as hard to you in telling your mind. Give her no token but stones, for she’s as hard as steel. |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | What said she? Nothing? |
| [[Speed\|SPEED]] | No, not so much as ‘Take this for thy pains’. To testify your bounty, I thank you, you have testerned me; in requital whereof, henceforth carry your letters yourself. And so, sir, I’ll commend you to my master. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Proteus\|PROTEUS]] | Go, go, be gone, to save your [[Ship\|ship]] from wreck,<br>Which cannot perish having thee aboard,<br>Being destined to a drier [[Death\|death]] on shore.<br>I must go send some better messenger.<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] my [[Julia]] would not deign my lines,<br>Receiving them from such a worthless post. Exit |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Entrano [[Valentine|Valentino]] e [[Proteus|Proteo]] |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | Cessa di persuadermi, mio amoroso [[Proteus|Proteo]].<br>Giovinezza [[Home|casalinga]] ebbe sempre casalingo ingegno.<br>Se l'affetto non [[Chains|incatenasse]] i tuoi teneri giorni<br>ai dolci sguardi del tuo onorato [[Love|amore]],<br>vorrei piuttosto pregarti di venir meco<br>a veder le meraviglie del mondo lontano,<br>anziché, vivendo qui infingardo e ozioso in [[Home|casa]],<br>logorar la tua giovinezza in informe pigrizia.<br>Ma poiché ami, séguita ad [[Love|amare]], e prospera in ciò—<br>come vorrei per me, quando ad [[Love|amare]] comincerò. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Vuoi dunque partire? Dolce [[Valentine|Valentino]], addio.<br>Pensa al tuo [[Proteus|Proteo]] quando per ventura scorgerai<br>qualche raro e notevole oggetto nel tuo viaggio.<br>Desiderami partecipe della tua felicità<br>quando t'incontri in buona sorte; e nel tuo pericolo—<br>se mai pericolo t'assedi—<br>affida i tuoi affanni alle mie sante preghiere;<br>ché io sarò il tuo orante, [[Valentine|Valentino]]. |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | E su un libro d'[[Love|amore]] pregherai per il mio [[success|successo]]? |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Su qualche libro che [[Love|amo]] pregherò per te. |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | Cioè su qualche superficiale storia di profondo [[Love|amore]]—<br>di come il giovane Leandro varcò l'Ellesponto. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | È profonda storia d'un più profondo [[Love|amore]],<br>ché ne fu immerso più su delle scarpe, nell'[[Love|amore]]. |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | È vero, ché voi ne siete immerso più su degli stivali, nell'[[Love|amore]],<br>e pure non nuotaste mai l'Ellesponto. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Più su degli stivali? Eh via, non calzarmi tu il piede. |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | No, non lo farò; ché a te non calza. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Che cosa? |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | L'esser in [[Love|amore]], dove il disprezzo si compra con gemiti,<br>gli sguardi schivi con sospiri che accorano, l'allegrezza d'un<br>attimo fuggente<br>con venti notti vigili, stanche, tediose.<br>Se per ventura la vinci, forse è guadagno sventurato;<br>se la perdi, ebbene, è una grave fatica guadagnata;<br>comunque sia, non è che follia comprata con senno,<br>o senno dalla follia soggiogato. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Così, per il vostro argomentare, mi chiamate [[Fool|folle]]. |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | Così, per il vostro argomentare, [[Fear|temo]] che tale vi mostrerete. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | È l'[[Love|amore]] che voi cavillate. Io non sono [[Love|amore]]. |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | [[Love|Amore]] è vostro padrone, ché vi signoreggia,<br>e chi è così aggiogato da un [[Fool|folle]]<br>non dovrebbe, a mio credere, esser registrato fra i savi. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Pure gli scrittori dicono: 'Come nel più dolce bocciolo<br>alberga il verme che lo rode, così l'[[Love|amore]] insensato<br>abita negl'ingegni più fini di tutti.' |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | E gli scrittori dicono: 'Come il bocciolo più precoce<br>è roso dal verme prima ch'egli sbocci,<br>così dall'[[Love|amore]] il giovane e tenero ingegno<br>è volto in follia, avvizzendo nel bocciolo,<br>perdendo il suo verde nel fiore stesso degli anni,<br>e ogni bel frutto di speranze future.'<br>Ma perché spreco io [[Time|tempo]] a consigliar te,<br>che sei devoto d'un folle [[Desire|desiderio]]?<br>Ancora una volta addio. Mio padre alla [[Road|rada]]<br>attende il mio venire, per vedermi imbarcato. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | E là io ti accompagnerò, [[Valentine|Valentino]]. |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | Dolce [[Proteus|Proteo]], no. Prendiamo ora congedo.<br>A Milano fammi saper di te per lettere<br>del tuo [[success|successo]] in [[Love|amore]], e d'ogni altra novella<br>che qui accada in assenza del tuo amico;<br>e io del pari ti visiterò con le mie. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Ti tocchi ogni felicità a Milano. |
| [[Valentine|VALENTINE]] | Altrettanto a voi in [[Home|patria]]; e così, addio. Esce |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Egli va a caccia d'[[Honour|onore]], io d'[[Love|amore]].<br>Egli [[Leaves|lascia]] gli amici per meglio nobilitarli,<br>io lascio me stesso, gli amici e tutto, per [[Love|amore]].<br>Tu, [[Julia|Giulia]], tu m'hai metamorfosato,<br>m'hai fatto trascurar gli studi, perdere il mio [[Time|tempo]],<br>far guerra al buon consiglio, tener per nulla il mondo;<br>fiacco l'ingegno col fantasticare, malato il cuore col pensiero. |
| *(didascalia)* | Entra [[Speed|Svelto]] |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Messer [[Proteus|Proteo]], Dio vi salvi. Avete visto il mio padrone? |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Or ora è partito di qui per imbarcarsi alla volta di Milano. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Venti contro uno, dunque, che è già imbarcato,<br>e io ho fatto la pecora, perdendolo. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Davvero, la pecora assai spesso si smarrisce,<br>se il pastore per un poco s'allontana. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Concludete dunque che il mio padrone è un pastore,<br>e io una pecora? |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Sì. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | E allora le mie corna sono le sue corna, ch'io vegli o [[Sleep|dorma]]. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Sciocca risposta, e che s'addice [[Well|bene]] a una pecora. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Questo prova che sono ancora una pecora. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Vero, e il tuo padrone un pastore. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | No, questo posso negarlo con un argomento. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Sarà ben difficile ch'io non lo provi con un altro. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Il pastore cerca la pecora, e non la pecora il pastore. Ma io cerco il mio padrone, e il mio padrone non cerca me. Dunque non sono una pecora. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Le pecore per il foraggio seguono il pastore, il pastore per il cibo non segue le pecore. Tu per il salario segui il tuo padrone, il tuo padrone per il salario non segue te. Dunque sei una pecora. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Un'altra prova come questa e mi farete gridare 'beee'. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Ma senti un po': hai dato la mia lettera a [[Julia|Giulia]]? |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Sì, messere. Io, montone smarrito, ho dato la vostra lettera a lei, agnella in busto; e lei, agnella in busto, non ha dato a me, montone smarrito, nulla per la mia fatica. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Ecco un pascolo troppo piccolo per tanta dovizia di montoni. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Se il terreno è sovraccarico, fareste meglio a sgozzarla. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | No, in questo tu vai errando. Sarebbe meglio metterti in libbra. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Eh no, messere: meno d'una libbra mi basterà per aver portato la vostra lettera. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Fraintendi. Intendo la libbra, cioè il serraglio delle bestie smarrite. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Da una libbra a uno spillo? Piegatelo e ripiegatelo: è tre volte troppo poco per aver portato una lettera alla vostra [[Lover|innamorata]]. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Ma che disse ella? |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | (fa di sì col capo, poi dice) Sì. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Capo-sì? Ebbene, questo fa 'capocchia'. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Avete frainteso, messere. Io dico che ella fece di sì col capo, e voi mi chiedete se fece di sì col capo, e io dico 'Sì'. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | E messo tutto insieme fa 'capocchia'. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Or che vi siete preso la pena di metterlo insieme, pigliatevelo per la vostra pena. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | No, no. L'avrai tu, per aver portato la lettera. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | [[Well|Bene]], vedo che dovrò rassegnarmi a portar pazienza con voi. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Come, messere, e in che modo porti con me? |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Perbacco, messere, la lettera l'ho portata assai per bene, non avendo per mia pena altro che la parola 'capocchia'. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Ch'io sia maledetto, ma tu hai l'ingegno pronto. |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | E pure non riesce a raggiungere la vostra borsa lenta. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Su, su, apri la faccenda in breve. Che disse ella? |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Aprite la vostra borsa, che il denaro e la faccenda sian consegnati a un tempo. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | (dandogli del denaro) [[Well|Bene]], messere, ecco per la tua pena. Che disse ella? |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | In verità, messere, credo che a stento la conquisterete. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Perché? Hai potuto ricavar tanto da lei? |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | Messere, non ho potuto ricavar nulla affatto da lei, no, neppure un ducato per aver recato la vostra lettera. Ed essendo stata così dura con me, che le portavo il vostro pensiero, [[Fear|temo]] che si mostrerà altrettanto dura con voi nel dichiararle il vostro pensiero. Non le date altro pegno che pietre, ché è dura come l'acciaio. |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Che disse ella? Nulla? |
| [[Speed|SPEED]] | No, neppure un 'Prendi questo per la tua pena'. A testimonio della vostra munificenza, vi ringrazio: mi avete testimoniato con un testone; in ricompensa di che, d'ora innanzi portatevi le lettere da voi. E così, messere, vi raccomanderò al mio padrone. |
| *(didascalia)* | Esce |
| [[Proteus|PROTEUS]] | Va', va', vattene, a salvare la tua [[Ship|nave]] dal naufragio,<br>ché non può perire avendo te a bordo,<br>essendo tu destinato a più asciutta [[Death|morte]] in terra.<br>Devo andare a mandare un miglior messaggero.<br>[[Fear|Temo]] che la mia [[Julia|Giulia]] non degnerebbe i miei versi,<br>ricevendoli da un così spregevole corriere. Esce |



<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_2" data-title="The Two Gentlemen of Verona — Act I, Scene 2" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/fool,motif/bees,motif/fire,motif/love,motif/tears,motif/time,motif/well,motif/wind,concept/body,concept/revenge,setting/verona,character/julia,character/nurse,character/proteus,character/valentine"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Julia]] and Lucetta |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | But say, Lucetta, now we are [[Alone\|alone]]—<br>Wouldst thou then counsel me to fall in [[Love\|love]]? |
| LUCETTA | Ay, madam, so you stumble not unheedfully. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Of all the fair resort of gentlemen<br>That every day with parle encounter me,<br>In thy opinion which is worthiest [[Love\|love]]? |
| LUCETTA | Please you repeat their names, I’ll show my mind<br>According to my shallow simple skill. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | What think’st thou of the fair Sir Eglamour? |
| LUCETTA | As of a knight [[Well\|well]] spoken, neat, and fine,<br>But were I you, he never should be mine. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | What think’st thou of the rich Mercatio? |
| LUCETTA | [[Well]] of his wealth, but of himself, so-so. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | What think’st thou of the gentle [[Proteus]]? |
| LUCETTA | Lord, lord, to see what folly reigns in us! |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | How now? What means this passion at his name? |
| LUCETTA | Pardon, dear madam, ‘tis a passing shame<br>That I, unworthy [[Body\|body]] as I am,<br>Should censure thus on lovely gentlemen. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Why not on [[Proteus]], as of all the rest? |
| LUCETTA | Then thus: of many good, I think him best. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Your reason? |
| LUCETTA | I have no other but a woman’s reason:<br>I think him so because I think him so. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And wouldst thou have me cast my [[Love\|love]] on him? |
| LUCETTA | Ay, if you thought your [[Love\|love]] not cast away. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Why, he of all the rest hath never moved me. |
| LUCETTA | Yet he of all the rest I think best loves ye. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | His little speaking shows his [[Love\|love]] but small. |
| LUCETTA | [[Fire]] that’s closest kept burns most of all. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | They do not [[Love\|love]] that do not show their [[Love\|love]]. |
| LUCETTA | O, they [[Love\|love]] least that let men know their [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | I would I knew his mind. |
| LUCETTA | (giving [[Proteus]]’ letter)<br>Peruse this paper, madam. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | ‘To [[Julia]]’—say, from whom? |
| LUCETTA | That the contents will show. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Say, say—who gave it thee? |
| LUCETTA | Sir [[Valentine]]’s page; and sent, I think, from [[Proteus]]. He would have given it you, but I being in the way Did in your name receive it. Pardon the fault, I pray. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Now, by my modesty, a goodly broker.<br>Dare you presume to harbour wanton lines?<br>To whisper, and conspire against my youth?<br>Now trust me, ‘tis an office of great worth,<br>And you an officer fit for the place.<br>There. Take the paper.<br>She gives Lucetta the letter<br>See it be returned,<br>Or else return no more into my sight. |
| LUCETTA | To plead for [[Love\|love]] deserves more fee than hate. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Will ye be gone? |
| LUCETTA | That you may ruminate. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And yet I would I had o‘erlooked the letter.<br>It were a shame to call her back again<br>And pray her to a fault for which I chid her.<br>What [[Fool\|fool]] is she, that knows I am a maid<br>And would not force the letter to my view,<br>Since maids in modesty say ‘No’ to that<br>Which they would have the profferer construe ‘Ay’.<br>Fie, fie, how wayward is this foolish [[Love\|love]]<br>That like a testy babe will scratch the [[Nurse\|nurse]]<br>And presently, all humbled, kiss the rod.<br>How churlishly I chid Lucetta hence<br>When willingly I would have had her here.<br>How angerly I taught my brow to frown<br>When inward joy enforced my heart to smile.<br>My penance is to call Lucetta back<br>And ask remission for my folly past.<br>What ho! Lucetta! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lucetta |
| LUCETTA | What would your ladyship? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Is’t near dinner-[[Time\|time]]? |
| LUCETTA | I would it were,<br>That you might kill your stomach on your meat<br>And not upon your maid. |
| *(didascalia)* | She drops and picks up the letter |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | What is’t that you<br>Took up so gingerly? |
| LUCETTA | Nothing. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Why didst thou stoop then? |
| LUCETTA | To take a paper up that I let fall. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And is that paper nothing? |
| LUCETTA | Nothing concerning me. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Then let it lie for those that it concerns. |
| LUCETTA | Madam, it will not lie where it concerns,<br>Unless it have a false interpreter. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Some [[Love\|love]] of yours hath writ to you in rhyme. |
| LUCETTA | That I might sing it, madam, to a tune,<br>Give me a note. Your ladyship can set. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | As little by such toys as may be possible.<br>Best sing it to the tune of ‘Light o’ [[Love\|love]]’. |
| LUCETTA | It is too heavy for so light a tune. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Heavy? Belike it hath some burden, then? |
| LUCETTA | Ay, and melodious were it, would you sing it. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | And why not you? |
| LUCETTA | I cannot reach so high. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Let’s see your [[Song\|song]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | She tries to take the letters |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | How now, minion! |
| LUCETTA | Keep tune there still. So you will sing it out.<br>And yet methinks I do not like this tune. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | You do not? |
| LUCETTA | No, madam, ‘tis too sharp. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | You, minion, are too saucy. |
| LUCETTA | Nay, now you are too flat,<br>And mar the concord with too harsh a descant.<br>There wanteth but a mean to fill your [[Song\|song]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | The mean is drowned with your unruly bass. |
| LUCETTA | Indeed, I bid the base for [[Proteus]]. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | This bauble shall not henceforth trouble me.<br>Here is a coil with protestation.<br>She [[Tears\|tears]] the letter and drops the pieces<br>Go, get you gone, and let the papers lie.<br>You would be fing’ring them to anger me. |
| LUCETTA | (aside)<br>She makes it strange, but she would be best pleased<br>To be so angered with another letter. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Nay, would I were so angered with the same.<br>O hateful hands, to tear such loving words;<br>Injurious wasps, to feed on such sweet honey<br>And kill the [[Bees\|bees]] that yield it with your stings.<br>I’ll kiss each several paper for amends.<br>She picks up some of the pieces of paper<br>Look, here is writ ‘Kind [[Julia]]’—unkind [[Julia]],<br>As in [[Revenge\|revenge]] of thy ingratitude<br>I throw thy name against the bruising stones,<br>Trampling contemptuously on thy disdain.<br>And here is writ ‘[[Love]]-wounded [[Proteus]]’.<br>Poor wounded name, my bosom as a bed<br>Shall lodge thee till thy wound be throughly healed;<br>And thus I search it with a sovereign kiss.<br>But twice or thrice was ‘[[Proteus]]’ written down.<br>Be calm, good [[Wind\|wind]], blow not a word away<br>Till I have found each letter in the letter<br>Except mine own name. That, some whirlwind bear<br>Unto a ragged, fearful, hanging rock<br>And throw it thence into the raging sea.<br>Lo, here in one line is his name twice writ:<br>‘Poor forlorn [[Proteus]]’, ‘passionate [[Proteus]]’,<br>‘To the sweet [[Julia]]’—that I’ll tear away.<br>And yet I will not, sith so prettily<br>He couples it to his complaining names.<br>Thus will I fold them, one upon another.<br>Now kiss, embrace, contend, do what you will. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lucetta |
| LUCETTA | Madam, dinner is ready, and your father stays. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | [[Well]], let us go. |
| LUCETTA | What, shall these papers lie like telltales here? |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | If you respect them, best to take them up. |
| LUCETTA | Nay, I was taken up for laying them down.<br>Yet here they shall not lie, for catching cold. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | I see you have a month’s mind to them. |
| LUCETTA | Ay, madam, you may say what sights you see.<br>I see things too, although you judge I wink. |
| [[Julia\|JULIA]] | Come, come, will’t please you go? |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Entrano [[Julia|Giulia]] e Lucetta |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Ma dimmi, Lucetta, ora che siamo [[Alone|sole]]—<br>mi consiglieresti tu di cadere in [[Love|amore]]? |
| LUCETTA | Sì, madonna, purché non inciampiate senza avvedervene. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Di tutta la bella schiera di gentiluomini<br>che ogni giorno mi vengono incontro a parlare,<br>chi è, a tuo parere, più degno d'[[Love|amore]]? |
| LUCETTA | Se vi piace ridirne i nomi, dirò il mio pensiero<br>secondo la mia povera e semplice capacità. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Che pensi del bel messer Eglamour? |
| LUCETTA | Che è cavaliere [[Well|ben]] parlante, lindo e garbato,<br>ma, s'io fossi in voi, non sarebbe mai il mio amato. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Che pensi del ricco Mercatio? |
| LUCETTA | [[Well|Bene]] della sua ricchezza, ma di lui, così così. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Che pensi del gentile [[Proteus|Proteo]]? |
| LUCETTA | Signore, Signore, a vedere che follia regna in noi! |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Che c'è ora? Che vuol dire questo turbamento al suo nome? |
| LUCETTA | Perdonate, cara madonna, è vergogna somma<br>ch'io, indegna [[Body|persona]] quale sono,<br>dia così giudizio su gentiluomini avvenenti. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Perché non su [[Proteus|Proteo]], come su tutti gli altri? |
| LUCETTA | Ebbene, ecco: fra molti buoni, lo stimo il migliore. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | La tua ragione? |
| LUCETTA | Non ne ho altra che una ragione di donna:<br>lo penso tale perché lo penso tale. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | E vorresti tu ch'io gettassi su di lui il mio [[Love|amore]]? |
| LUCETTA | Sì, se pensaste che il vostro [[Love|amore]] non fosse gettato via. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Ma egli, fra tutti gli altri, non m'ha mai fatto istanza. |
| LUCETTA | Eppure egli, fra tutti gli altri, credo che più v'ami. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Il suo poco parlare mostra il suo [[Love|amore]] ben piccolo. |
| LUCETTA | [[Fire|Fuoco]] più chiuso è quello che più divampa. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Non [[Love|amano]] coloro che non mostrano il loro [[Love|amore]]. |
| LUCETTA | Oh, [[Love|amano]] meno coloro che fanno saper altrui il loro [[Love|amore]]. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Vorrei conoscere il suo animo. |
| LUCETTA | (porgendo la lettera di [[Proteus|Proteo]])<br>Scorrete questo foglio, madonna. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | 'A [[Julia|Giulia]]'—dimmi, da parte di chi? |
| LUCETTA | Questo ve lo mostrerà il contenuto. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Dimmi, dimmi—chi te l'ha data? |
| LUCETTA | Il paggio di messer [[Valentine|Valentino]]; e mandata, credo, da [[Proteus|Proteo]]. Voleva darla a voi, ma trovandomi io sul cammino la ricevetti in vostro nome. Perdonate il fallo, ve ne prego. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Ora, per la mia modestia, una bella mezzana.<br>Osate voi accogliere righe lascive?<br>Bisbigliare e congiurare contro la mia giovinezza?<br>Credetemi, è un ufficio di gran pregio,<br>e voi un'ufficiale adatta al posto.<br>Ecco. Prendete il foglio.<br>Dà a Lucetta la lettera<br>Badate che sia restituito,<br>o altrimenti non tornate più al mio cospetto. |
| LUCETTA | Perorare per l'[[Love|amore]] merita più mercede che odio. |
| [[Julia|JULIA]] | Volete andarvene? |
| LUCETTA | Perché possiate ruminare. |
| *(didascalia)* | Esce |
